# 6.1 Introduction to Eigenvalues: $Ax = \lambda x$

Up to this point in linear algebra, we have lived in a static world governed by equilibrium equations like $Ax = b$. Whenever we wanted to solve $Ax = b$, Gaussian elimination was our go-to workhorse. Now, linear algebra enters a **dynamic realm** with the study of **eigenvalues** ($\lambda$) and **eigenvectors** ($x$). Instead of solving for a single static state, eigenvalues and eigenvectors govern systems of continuous and discrete *change*: discrete-time difference equations ($u_{k+1} = A u_k$) driven by matrix powers $A^k$, and continuous-time differential equations ($du/dt = Au$) driven by matrix exponentials $e^{At}$.

Why can't we use Gaussian elimination to solve these dynamic problems? Because row operations completely scramble the internal directions of a matrix! Instead of elimination, we look for the special, intrinsic coordinate axes of $A$—directions along which the matrix acts as nothing more than a simple scalar multiplier, stretching, shrinking, or flipping vectors without ever rotating them off their line of action.

## Fundamental Definitions and Geometric Meaning

What happens when you multiply almost any vector $v$ by a square matrix $A$? In general, the output vector $Av$ gets rotated and sheared into a completely new direction. The exceptions to this rule—the special non-zero vectors that refuse to change direction when multiplied by $A$—are the **eigenvectors**.

> [!abstract] Definition: Eigenvalues and Eigenvectors
> Let $A$ be an $n \times n$ square matrix. A non-zero vector $x \in \mathbb{C}^n$ (or $\mathbb{R}^n$) is called an **eigenvector** of $A$ if the linear transformation $A x$ produces a vector collinear with $x$:
> $$
> Ax = \lambda x \quad (x \ne 0)
> $$
> The scalar factor $\lambda$ is the **eigenvalue** associated with the eigenvector $x$.

![[06. Eigenvalues and Eigenvectors-1792294445890.webp]]

Notice why the definition explicitly insists on $x \ne 0$: the zero vector satisfies $A(0) = \lambda(0)$ for *every* scalar $\lambda$, which tells us nothing about the geometry of $A$. An eigenvector must be a genuine, non-zero direction in space, whereas the eigenvalue $\lambda$ is allowed to be any scalar—positive, negative, complex, or even $\lambda = 0$!

### Geometric Interpretation and Eigenspaces

Let's unpack what $Ax = \lambda x$ means geometrically:

1. **Direction Invariance:**
   While a typical vector $v$ is knocked off its line by $A$, an eigenvector $x$ is invariant in direction: multiplying $x$ by $A$ keeps it on the exact same line through the origin. It simply stretches $x$ if $|\lambda| > 1$, shrinks it if $|\lambda| < 1$, reverses its orientation if $\lambda < 0$, or collapses it to the origin if $\lambda = 0$.
2. **Eigenvector Lines and Eigenspaces:**
   What happens if we scale an eigenvector $x$ by any non-zero constant $c \ne 0$? By linearity, the entire line spanned by $x$ shares the exact same eigenvalue $\lambda$:
   $$
   A(cx) = c(Ax) = c(\lambda x) = \lambda(cx)
   $$
   Even better, if $x^{(1)}$ and $x^{(2)}$ are two eigenvectors sharing the same eigenvalue $\lambda$, then their sum also satisfies $A(x^{(1)} + x^{(2)}) = \lambda(x^{(1)} + x^{(2)})$. If we gather all eigenvectors corresponding to a specific $\lambda$ together with the zero vector $0$ (to include the origin), they form a genuine subspace called the **eigenspace** of $\lambda$. Writing $Ax = \lambda x$ as $(A - \lambda I)x = 0$ reveals that this eigenspace is simply the nullspace of the shifted matrix $A - \lambda I$:
   $$
   E_\lambda = N(A - \lambda I)
   $$

### Eigenvector Decomposition and Decoupled Evolution

Why are eigenvectors the secret weapon for solving dynamic problems? Ask yourself: *What happens if we apply $A$ repeatedly to an arbitrary starting vector $v$?*

Computing $A^{100} v$ directly by multiplying an $n \times n$ matrix a hundred times is both tedious and opaque. Suppose instead that we can express our initial vector $v$ as a linear combination of eigenvectors $x_1, x_2, \dots, x_k$ with associated eigenvalues $\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \dots, \lambda_k$:
$$
v = c_1 x_1 + c_2 x_2 + \dots + c_k x_k
$$
When we multiply $v$ by $A$, linearity allows $A$ to act on each eigenvector component independently. Along each eigenvector axis $x_i$, matrix multiplication turns into simple scalar multiplication by $\lambda_i$:
$$
Av = c_1 Ax_1 + c_2 Ax_2 + \dots + c_k Ax_k = c_1 \lambda_1 x_1 + c_2 \lambda_2 x_2 + \dots + c_k \lambda_k x_k
$$
Look at what happens when we apply $A$ again, and again, $n$ times in a row—each eigenvector component simply gets multiplied by $\lambda_i$ at every step:
$$
A^n v = c_1 \lambda_1^n x_1 + c_2 \lambda_2^n x_2 + \dots + c_k \lambda_k^n x_k
$$
In this basis of eigenvectors, the coupled matrix system completely **decouples** into independent scalar evolutions along each eigenvector direction! In Section 6.2, we will formalize this exact idea into a full matrix factorization ($A = S \Lambda S^{-1}$), which will let us compute matrix powers $A^n$ and solve systems of difference equations in a single stroke.

> [!note] Dynamic Modes and Asymptotic Growth
> Because $A^n v$ is governed by the scalar powers $\lambda_i^n$, the magnitudes of the eigenvalues $|\lambda_i|$ completely dictate the long-term fate of discrete dynamical systems as $n \to \infty$:
> - **Decaying Modes ($|\lambda_i| < 1$):** As $n \to \infty$, $\lambda_i^n \to 0$. Any initial component along $x_i$ dies out asymptotically.
> - **Steady State ($\lambda_i = 1$):** As $n \to \infty$, $\lambda_i^n = 1$. The component along $x_i$ remains permanently unchanged, forming the long-term equilibrium of the system.
> - **Unbounded Growth ($|\lambda_i| > 1$):** As $n \to \infty$, $|\lambda_i|^n \to \infty$. The system blows up exponentially along the direction of $x_i$.

![[06. Eigenvalues and Eigenvectors-1792031756777.webp]]

## The Characteristic Equation: $\det(A - \lambda I) = 0$

When we can't spot the eigenvectors of a matrix by geometric inspection, how do we find $\lambda$ and $x$ systematically? Notice that in $Ax = \lambda x$, *both* $\lambda$ and $x$ are unknown, and they are multiplied together! To separate them, we bring everything to the left-hand side.

Why do we introduce the identity matrix $I$ and shift $A$ by $\lambda I$? Because you cannot subtract a scalar $\lambda$ from a matrix $A$—the expression "$A - \lambda$" makes no algebraic sense. Writing $\lambda x$ as $\lambda I x$ lets us factor out the vector $x$ cleanly:
$$
Ax = \lambda x \iff (A - \lambda I)x = 0
$$
Look closely at what this equation says: an eigenvector $x$ is a **non-zero** vector in the nullspace of the shifted matrix $(A - \lambda I)$. That observation gives us the fundamental test for finding every eigenvalue of $A$ *before* we know any of its eigenvectors.

> [!theorem] The Eigenvalue Condition
> A scalar $\lambda$ is an eigenvalue of $A$ if and only if the shifted matrix $(A - \lambda I)$ is singular:
> $$
> \det(A - \lambda I) = 0
> $$

**Proof:**
By definition, an eigenvector $x$ must be non-zero ($x \ne 0$). The homogeneous linear system $(A - \lambda I)x = 0$ admits a non-trivial solution $x \ne 0$ if and only if the nullspace $N(A - \lambda I)$ contains more than just the zero vector. By the Fundamental Theorem of Linear Algebra and our invertibility criteria from earlier chapters, a square matrix has a non-trivial nullspace if and only if it is non-invertible (singular), which is equivalent to having a zero determinant: $\det(A - \lambda I) = 0$. $\blacksquare$

> [!abstract] Definition: Characteristic Polynomial
> For an $n \times n$ matrix $A$, the determinant:
> $$
> p(\lambda) = \det(A - \lambda I)
> $$
> is a polynomial in $\lambda$ of degree $n$, called the **characteristic polynomial** of $A$. Setting it to zero gives the **characteristic equation** $\det(A - \lambda I) = 0$.

Why is $p(\lambda) = \det(A - \lambda I)$ guaranteed to be a polynomial of degree $n$? When you expand the determinant of $(A - \lambda I)$, the product down the main diagonal is $(a_{11} - \lambda)(a_{22} - \lambda)\cdots(a_{nn} - \lambda)$, which contributes $(-1)^n \lambda^n$ as the highest-power term. By the **Fundamental Theorem of Algebra**, every polynomial of degree $n$ with complex coefficients factors completely into $n$ linear factors over $\mathbb{C}$:
$$
\det(A - \lambda I) = (-1)^n (\lambda - \lambda_1)(\lambda - \lambda_2)\cdots(\lambda - \lambda_n) = 0
$$
Thus, every $n \times n$ matrix has exactly $n$ eigenvalues $\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \dots, \lambda_n$ in the complex plane, counted with their **algebraic multiplicities** (how many times a root repeats in the polynomial factorization).

Keep in mind, however, that repeated roots can introduce a subtle wrinkle: while a root $\lambda$ might repeat $k$ times in the characteristic polynomial, the nullspace $N(A - \lambda I)$ might sometimes yield *fewer* than $k$ linearly independent eigenvectors! When a repeated eigenvalue fails to produce enough independent eigenvectors to match its algebraic multiplicity, the matrix is called **defective**. We will explore this phenomenon in detail—and prove the exact inequality relating algebraic and geometric multiplicities—in Section 6.2 when we study matrix diagonalization.

### The Three-Step Algorithm for Finding Eigensystems

Putting these pieces together gives us a concrete three-step recipe for computing the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of small matrices by hand:

1. **Form the Characteristic Polynomial:** Subtract $\lambda$ from the entries along the main diagonal of $A$ and expand the determinant $\det(A - \lambda I)$.
2. **Solve for the Eigenvalues:** Find the $n$ roots $\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_n$ of the polynomial equation $\det(A - \lambda I) = 0$.
3. **Solve for the Eigenvectors:** For each distinct eigenvalue $\lambda_i$, plug that number back into $(A - \lambda_i I)x = 0$ and use Gaussian elimination to find a basis for the nullspace $N(A - \lambda_i I)$.

> [!note] Shortcut for $2 \times 2$ Eigenvectors
> When $\lambda$ is an eigenvalue of a $2 \times 2$ matrix $A$, the shifted matrix $(A - \lambda I)$ is guaranteed to be singular, so its two rows must be scalar multiples of each other. If a non-zero row of $(A - \lambda I)$ is $\begin{bmatrix} a & b \end{bmatrix}$, you don't even need elimination to write down an eigenvector in its nullspace—just swap the entries and negate one of them:
> $$
> x = \begin{bmatrix} -b \\ a \end{bmatrix} \quad \text{or} \quad \begin{bmatrix} b \\ -a \end{bmatrix}
> $$
> You can see immediately that the dot product with the row vanishes: $\begin{bmatrix} a & b \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} -b \\ a \end{bmatrix} = -ab + ba = 0$.

## Core Algebraic Properties of the Eigensystem

One of the most rewarding aspects of eigenvalues is how predictably they behave when you transform a matrix $A$ into $A^n$, $A^{-1}$, $A + cI$, or $A^T$. Let's walk through six foundational properties of the eigensystem and see why each one works.

### Property 1: Powers of a Matrix

What happens to the eigenvalues and eigenvectors when we square or cube a matrix? Because $A$ scales its eigenvector $x$ by $\lambda$ without changing its direction, applying $A$ a second time scales that same vector $x$ by another factor of $\lambda$.

> [!theorem] Eigenvalues of Matrix Powers
> If $x$ is an eigenvector of $A$ with eigenvalue $\lambda$, then for every positive integer $n \in \mathbb{N}$, $x$ remains an eigenvector of $A^n$ with eigenvalue $\lambda^n$:
> $$
> A^n x = \lambda^n x
> $$

**Proof:**
We proceed by mathematical induction. The base case $n = 1$ is the defining relation $Ax = \lambda x$. Assuming the statement holds for $n = k$, so that $A^k x = \lambda^k x$, we multiply both sides on the left by $A$:
$$
A^{k+1} x = A(A^k x) = A(\lambda^k x) = \lambda^k (Ax) = \lambda^k (\lambda x) = \lambda^{k+1} x
$$
Thus, $A^n$ keeps the exact same eigenvectors $x$ while raising every eigenvalue to the $n$-th power. $\blacksquare$

### Property 2: Inverses of Matrices

This pattern extends naturally to negative powers when $A$ is invertible: if multiplying by $A$ stretches $x$ by $\lambda$, then multiplying by $A^{-1}$ must undo that stretch by scaling $x$ by $1/\lambda$.

> [!theorem] Eigenvalues of the Inverse Matrix
> If $A$ is invertible, then every eigenvalue satisfies $\lambda \ne 0$, and $x$ is an eigenvector of $A^{-1}$ with eigenvalue $\lambda^{-1}$:
> $$
> A^{-1} x = \frac{1}{\lambda} x
> $$

**Proof:**
Starting from $Ax = \lambda x$ (with $x \ne 0$), multiply both sides on the left by $A^{-1}$:
$$
A^{-1}(Ax) = A^{-1}(\lambda x) \implies x = \lambda (A^{-1}x)
$$
Because $A$ is invertible, its nullspace is trivial, which guarantees $\lambda \ne 0$ (otherwise $Ax = 0$ for $x \ne 0$). Dividing both sides by the non-zero scalar $\lambda$ yields:
$$
A^{-1} x = \frac{1}{\lambda} x
$$
Once again, the eigenvector $x$ is completely unchanged! $\blacksquare$

### Property 3: Diagonal Shift

We already used the shifted matrix $A - \lambda I$ to build the characteristic equation. More generally, what happens if we add any scalar multiple of the identity matrix, $cI$, to $A$? Because the identity matrix $I$ treats every vector in space as an eigenvector ($Ix = x$), adding $cI$ shifts every eigenvalue by $c$ without disturbing a single eigenvector.

> [!theorem] Diagonal Shift Theorem
> If $A$ has eigenvalue $\lambda$ corresponding to eigenvector $x$, then $(A + cI)$ has eigenvalue $\lambda + c$ corresponding to the exact same eigenvector $x$:
> $$
> (A + cI)x = (\lambda + c)x
> $$

**Proof:**
Using the distributivity of matrix-vector multiplication and the identity $Ix = x$, we have:
$$
(A + cI)x = Ax + cIx = \lambda x + cx = (\lambda + c)x
$$
This confirms that shifting the main diagonal of $A$ by $c$ shifts its entire spectrum by $c$ while keeping all eigenvectors intact. $\blacksquare$

### Property 4: Singular Matrices and Zero Eigenvalues

Earlier we noted that an eigenvector can never be zero, but an eigenvalue *can* be zero. When does $\lambda = 0$ occur?

> [!theorem] Zero Eigenvalues and Singularity
> A square matrix $A$ is singular (non-invertible) if and only if $\lambda = 0$ is an eigenvalue of $A$.

**Proof:**
By definition, $A$ is singular if and only if $\det(A) = 0$, which we can write as $\det(A - 0 \cdot I) = 0$. That is precisely the condition that $\lambda = 0$ satisfies the characteristic equation! Equivalently, plug $\lambda = 0$ directly into the eigenvalue equation:
$$
Ax = 0 \cdot x = 0 \iff x \in N(A)
$$
The eigenspace corresponding to $\lambda = 0$ is literally the nullspace $N(A)$, and the number of independent eigenvectors for $\lambda = 0$ (its geometric multiplicity) is the nullity $\dim N(A) = n - \operatorname{rank}(A)$. $\blacksquare$

### Property 5: Triangular Matrices

In general, finding eigenvalues requires solving a degree-$n$ polynomial equation. Is there a class of matrices where the eigenvalues are sitting right in front of us in plain sight? Yes—whenever a matrix is **triangular** (upper triangular, lower triangular, or diagonal), its eigenvalues are the entries right on the main diagonal!

> [!theorem] Spectrum of a Triangular Matrix
> For any upper triangular or lower triangular matrix $U$, the eigenvalues are precisely its main diagonal entries:
> $$
> \lambda_i = u_{ii} \quad (i = 1, 2, \dots, n)
> $$

**Proof:**
If $U$ is upper (or lower) triangular, then subtracting $\lambda$ from the diagonal preserves the triangular structure, leaving $(U - \lambda I)$ with diagonal entries $(u_{11} - \lambda), (u_{22} - \lambda), \dots, (u_{nn} - \lambda)$. Because the determinant of any triangular matrix is the product of its diagonal entries, the characteristic equation is already factored for us:
$$
\det(U - \lambda I) = (u_{11} - \lambda)(u_{22} - \lambda)\cdots(u_{nn} - \lambda) = 0
$$
Reading off the roots immediately gives $\lambda_i = u_{ii}$. $\blacksquare$

> [!warning] Row Operations Do NOT Preserve Eigenvalues
> Seeing that the eigenvalues of $U$ sit on its main diagonal tempts many students into a dangerous trap: *"Can I just run Gaussian elimination to turn $A$ into an upper triangular matrix $U$, and then read the eigenvalues of $A$ off the diagonal of $U$?"*
> 
> **Absolutely not!** While row operations preserve the nullspace $N(A)$ and the row space, elimination **destroys eigenvalues**:
> $$
> A \xrightarrow{\text{elimination}} U \centernot\implies \operatorname{eig}(A) = \operatorname{eig}(U)
> $$
> The diagonal entries of $U$ are the **pivots** of $A$, and pivots are generally completely different from the eigenvalues of $A$. Subtracting a multiple of row $1$ from row $2$ changes the trace and mixes coordinates in a way that alters the roots of $\det(A - \lambda I) = 0$.

### Property 6: Spectrum of the Transpose ($A$ and $A^T$)

How do the eigenvalues of the transpose $A^T$ compare to the eigenvalues of $A$?

> [!theorem] Transpose Spectrum Theorem
> A square matrix $A$ and its transpose $A^T$ have identical characteristic polynomials and therefore share the exact same eigenvalues (with the same algebraic multiplicities):
> $$
> \operatorname{eig}(A^T) = \operatorname{eig}(A)
> $$

**Proof:**
Since transposing the identity matrix leaves it unchanged ($I^T = I$), the matrix $(A^T - \lambda I)$ is simply the transpose of $(A - \lambda I)$. Using the fundamental rule that a matrix and its transpose have the same determinant, $\det(M^T) = \det(M)$, we find:
$$
\det(A^T - \lambda I) = \det\left((A - \lambda I)^T\right) = \det(A - \lambda I)
$$
Because the characteristic polynomials $p_{A^T}(\lambda)$ and $p_A(\lambda)$ are identical, their roots—and the multiplicities of those roots—must match completely. $\blacksquare$

### Right and Left Eigenvectors

Even though $A$ and $A^T$ share the exact same eigenvalues $\lambda$, they generally do **not** share the same eigenvectors (unless $A$ happens to be symmetric or normal). After all, the eigenvectors of $A$ live in the nullspace $N(A - \lambda I)$, while the eigenvectors of $A^T$ live in the *left nullspace* $N(A^T - \lambda I)$! Because multiplying an eigenvector of $A^T$ on the left of $A$ produces $(A^T y)^T = y^T A = \lambda y^T$, we give these row eigenvectors a special name.

> [!abstract] Definition: Right and Left Eigenvectors
> Let $A$ be an $n \times n$ matrix with eigenvalue $\lambda$:
> 1. **Right Eigenvectors (Standard):**
>    A non-zero column vector $x \in \mathbb{C}^n$ satisfying:
>    $$
>    Ax = \lambda x
>    $$
>    is a standard (or **right**) eigenvector of $A$, residing in the nullspace $N(A - \lambda I)$.
> 2. **Left Eigenvectors:**
>    A non-zero row vector $y^T \in \mathbb{C}^{1 \times n}$ (or column vector $y \in \mathbb{C}^n$) satisfying:
>    $$
>    y^T A = \lambda y^T \iff A^T y = \lambda y
>    $$
>    is a **left eigenvector** of $A$ associated with eigenvalue $\lambda$. It is an ordinary right eigenvector of $A^T$, residing in the left nullspace $N(A^T - \lambda I) = N\left((A - \lambda I)^T\right)$.

> [!note] Biorthogonality of Left and Right Eigenvectors
> Even when $A$ is not symmetric—so its right eigenvectors $x_1, \dots, x_n$ are not orthogonal to one another—there is a remarkable hidden orthogonality between the **left** eigenvectors and the **right** eigenvectors: whenever $\lambda_i \ne \lambda_j$ are distinct eigenvalues, the left eigenvector $y_i^T$ of $\lambda_i$ and the right eigenvector $x_j$ of $\lambda_j$ are **mutually orthogonal**!
> $$
> y_i^T x_j = 0 \quad (\text{if } \lambda_i \ne \lambda_j)
> $$
> 
> **Proof:**
> We evaluate the scalar product $y_i^T A x_j$ in two different ways by shifting the parentheses. Grouping $(y_i^T A)$ first uses the left eigenvector equation $y_i^T A = \lambda_i y_i^T$:
> $$
> (y_i^T A) x_j = (\lambda_i y_i^T) x_j = \lambda_i (y_i^T x_j)
> $$
> Grouping $(A x_j)$ first uses the right eigenvector equation $A x_j = \lambda_j x_j$:
> $$
> y_i^T (A x_j) = y_i^T (\lambda_j x_j) = \lambda_j (y_i^T x_j)
> $$
> Since both expressions equal the exact same number $y_i^T A x_j$, their difference must be zero:
> $$
> \lambda_i (y_i^T x_j) = \lambda_j (y_i^T x_j) \implies (\lambda_i - \lambda_j)(y_i^T x_j) = 0
> $$
> Because the eigenvalues are distinct ($\lambda_i - \lambda_j \ne 0$), we are forced to conclude that $y_i^T x_j = 0$.

> [!warning] The Fallacy of Naive Addition and Multiplication ($A + B$ and $AB$)
> Having seen that $(A + cI)$ has eigenvalues $\lambda + c$ and $A^2$ has eigenvalues $\lambda^2$, it is very tempting to guess that for two arbitrary $n \times n$ matrices $A$ and $B$:
> - Are the eigenvalues of $A + B$ equal to $\lambda_A + \lambda_B$? **No!**
> - Are the eigenvalues of $AB$ equal to $\lambda_A \lambda_B$? **No!**
> 
> Why do both formulas fail? Look at what goes wrong when you try to write $ABx = A(\beta x) = \beta(Ax) = \beta \lambda x$, or $(A + B)x = Ax + Bx = (\lambda + \beta)x$: both steps quietly assume that the **exact same vector $x$** is simultaneously an eigenvector of $A$ *and* an eigenvector of $B$! For two typical matrices $A$ and $B$, their eigenvector axes point in completely different directions, so $Bx$ is rotated off the eigenvector line of $A$, and the eigenvalues of $A + B$ or $AB$ bear no simple relation to $\lambda_A$ and $\lambda_B$.
> 
> *(When **do** two matrices $A$ and $B$ share the exact same eigenvectors so that their eigenvalues add and multiply cleanly? In Section 6.2, once we develop matrix diagonalization, we will prove a celebrated theorem: two diagonalizable matrices share a complete basis of eigenvectors if and only if they **commute**, meaning $AB = BA$!)*

## Trace and Determinant of Matrices

Solving a degree-$n$ characteristic polynomial can be hard work, so it is natural to ask: *Can we check our eigenvalues—or extract global information about the spectrum—directly from the entries of $A$ without solving $\det(A - \lambda I) = 0$?*

Two scalar invariants of a matrix $A$ give us immediate, exact links between the matrix entries and the eigenvalues: the **determinant** and the **trace**.

> [!abstract] Definition: Trace of a Matrix
> The **trace** of an $n \times n$ square matrix $A$, denoted $\operatorname{tr}(A)$, is the sum of the entries along its main diagonal:
> $$
> \operatorname{tr}(A) = \sum_{i=1}^n a_{ii} = a_{11} + a_{22} + \dots + a_{nn}
> $$

### Algebraic Properties of the Trace

Before connecting the trace to eigenvalues, let's establish two simple, indispensable algebraic properties of $\operatorname{tr}(A)$: it is a **linear functional**, and it is **cyclically invariant** under multiplication.

> [!theorem] Linearity of the Trace
> For any $n \times n$ matrices $A, B \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n}$ and scalar $c \in \mathbb{C}$, the trace is a linear mapping:
> $$
> \operatorname{tr}(A \pm B) = \operatorname{tr}(A) \pm \operatorname{tr}(B)
> $$
> $$
> \operatorname{tr}(cA) = c \, \operatorname{tr}(A)
> $$

**Proof:**
By the definition of matrix addition, the $i^{\text{th}}$ diagonal entry of $(A \pm B)$ is $(A \pm B)_{ii} = a_{ii} \pm b_{ii}$. Summing over all $n$ diagonal positions gives:
$$
\operatorname{tr}(A \pm B) = \sum_{i=1}^n (a_{ii} \pm b_{ii}) = \sum_{i=1}^n a_{ii} \pm \sum_{i=1}^n b_{ii} = \operatorname{tr}(A) \pm \operatorname{tr}(B)
$$
Similarly, scaling $A$ by $c$ multiplies each diagonal entry by $c$:
$$
\operatorname{tr}(cA) = \sum_{i=1}^n c a_{ii} = c \sum_{i=1}^n a_{ii} = c \, \operatorname{tr}(A)
$$
Hence the trace is linear. $\blacksquare$

What about matrix multiplication? Even though matrix multiplication is famously non-commutative ($AB \ne BA$ in general), a remarkable miracle occurs on the main diagonal: the sum of the diagonal entries of $AB$ is *always* equal to the sum of the diagonal entries of $BA$!

> [!theorem] Cyclic Invariance: $\operatorname{tr}(AB) = \operatorname{tr}(BA)$
> For any matrices $A \in \mathbb{C}^{m \times n}$ and $B \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times m}$, the square product matrices $AB$ ($m \times m$) and $BA$ ($n \times n$) have the exact same trace:
> $$
> \operatorname{tr}(AB) = \operatorname{tr}(BA)
> $$

**Proof:**
By the definition of matrix multiplication, the $i^{\text{th}}$ diagonal entry of the $m \times m$ matrix $AB$ is the dot product of row $i$ of $A$ with column $i$ of $B$: $(AB)_{ii} = \sum_{j=1}^n a_{ij} b_{ji}$. Summing over all $i = 1, \dots, m$ gives:
$$
\operatorname{tr}(AB) = \sum_{i=1}^m (AB)_{ii} = \sum_{i=1}^m \sum_{j=1}^n a_{ij} b_{ji}
$$
Now compute the trace of the $n \times n$ matrix $BA$ in the same way. Its $j^{\text{th}}$ diagonal entry is $(BA)_{jj} = \sum_{i=1}^m b_{ji} a_{ij}$, and summing over $j = 1, \dots, n$ gives:
$$
\operatorname{tr}(BA) = \sum_{j=1}^n (BA)_{jj} = \sum_{j=1}^n \sum_{i=1}^m b_{ji} a_{ij}
$$
Because individual numbers commute ($a_{ij} b_{ji} = b_{ji} a_{ij}$) and we can interchange the order of two finite summations, both expressions sum the exact same $mn$ pairwise products:
$$
\operatorname{tr}(AB) = \sum_{i=1}^m \sum_{j=1}^n a_{ij} b_{ji} = \sum_{j=1}^n \sum_{i=1}^m b_{ji} a_{ij} = \operatorname{tr}(BA)
$$
Thus $\operatorname{tr}(AB) = \operatorname{tr}(BA)$. $\blacksquare$

### Product of Eigenvalues

Now let's connect the determinant and the trace directly to the eigenvalues $\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_n$. How does $\det(A)$ relate to the eigenvalues?

> [!theorem] Determinant as the Product of Eigenvalues
> For any $n \times n$ matrix $A$ with eigenvalues $\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \dots, \lambda_n$ (counted with algebraic multiplicity):
> $$
> \det(A) = \prod_{i=1}^n \lambda_i = \lambda_1 \lambda_2 \cdots \lambda_n
> $$

**Proof:**
By the Fundamental Theorem of Algebra, the characteristic polynomial factors into its $n$ roots for every value of the variable $\lambda$:
$$
\det(A - \lambda I) = (-1)^n (\lambda - \lambda_1)(\lambda - \lambda_2)\cdots(\lambda - \lambda_n)
$$
Because this is an identity between two polynomials in $\lambda$, it must hold when we plug in any specific number for $\lambda$. Let's set $\lambda = 0$ on both sides! On the left side, $A - 0 \cdot I$ simplifies to $A$. On the right side, each factor $(0 - \lambda_i)$ contributes a minus sign that cancels the $(-1)^n$ in front:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\det(A - 0 \cdot I) &= (-1)^n (0 - \lambda_1)(0 - \lambda_2)\cdots(0 - \lambda_n) \\
\det(A) &= (-1)^n (-\lambda_1)(-\lambda_2)\cdots(-\lambda_n) \\
&= (-1)^n (-1)^n (\lambda_1 \lambda_2 \cdots \lambda_n) \\
&= (-1)^{2n} \prod_{i=1}^n \lambda_i = \prod_{i=1}^n \lambda_i
\end{aligned}
$$
Thus, the determinant of $A$ is always the product of its $n$ eigenvalues. $\blacksquare$

### Sum of Eigenvalues

While setting $\lambda = 0$ in the characteristic polynomial gave us the constant term $\det(A)$, what happens if we compare the *next-to-highest* degree term (the coefficient of $\lambda^{n-1}$) in $\det(A - \lambda I)$? That comparison yields one of the most useful checks in all of linear algebra.

> [!theorem] Trace as the Sum of Eigenvalues
> For any $n \times n$ matrix $A$ with eigenvalues $\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \dots, \lambda_n$ (counted with algebraic multiplicity):
> $$
> \operatorname{tr}(A) = \sum_{i=1}^n \lambda_i = \lambda_1 + \lambda_2 + \dots + \lambda_n
> $$

**Proof:**
Let's examine the coefficient of $(-\lambda)^{n-1}$ in the characteristic polynomial $\det(A - \lambda I)$ by expanding it in two different ways:

1. **From the Root Factorization:**
   Writing $\det(A - \lambda I) = \prod_{i=1}^n (\lambda_i - \lambda)$, we expand the product in decreasing powers of $(-\lambda)$. To form a term of degree $(-\lambda)^{n-1}$, we choose $(-\lambda)$ from $n - 1$ of the factors and $\lambda_i$ from the remaining factor:
   $$
   \det(A - \lambda I) = \prod_{i=1}^n (\lambda_i - \lambda) = (-\lambda)^n + \left(\sum_{i=1}^n \lambda_i\right)(-\lambda)^{n-1} + \dots + \prod_{i=1}^n \lambda_i
   $$
2. **From the Big Formula (Leibniz Expansion) for the Determinant:**
   Recall that the determinant of $(A - \lambda I)$ is a sum over all $n!$ permutations, where each term selects one entry from each row and column. The main diagonal product is:
   $$
   (a_{11} - \lambda)(a_{22} - \lambda)\cdots(a_{nn} - \lambda) = (-\lambda)^n + \left(\sum_{i=1}^n a_{ii}\right)(-\lambda)^{n-1} + \mathcal{O}(\lambda^{n-2})
   $$
   Why can't any *other* permutation contribute to $(-\lambda)^n$ or $(-\lambda)^{n-1}$? Because if you step off the main diagonal in one row to pick an off-diagonal entry $a_{ij}$ ($i \ne j$), you have now used up both row $i$ and column $j$—meaning you miss *at least two* diagonal entries $(a_{ii} - \lambda)$ and $(a_{jj} - \lambda)$! Consequently, every non-diagonal permutation product contains at most $n - 2$ factors of $\lambda$ and has degree at most $n - 2$.

Equating the coefficients of $(-\lambda)^{n-1}$ from both expansions forces the sum of the eigenvalues to equal the sum of the diagonal entries:
$$
\sum_{i=1}^n \lambda_i = \sum_{i=1}^n a_{ii} = \operatorname{tr}(A)
$$
Even though individual diagonal entries are *not* eigenvalues (unless $A$ is triangular), their sum is guaranteed to equal the sum of all the eigenvalues! $\blacksquare$

### Characteristic Equation for $2 \times 2$ Matrices

For a $2 \times 2$ matrix $A = \begin{bmatrix} a & b \\ c & d \end{bmatrix}$, the trace $\operatorname{tr}(A) = a + d$ and the determinant $\det(A) = ad - bc$ tell the *entire* story of the characteristic polynomial. Expanding $\det(A - \lambda I)$ directly gives:
$$
\det(A - \lambda I) = (a - \lambda)(d - \lambda) - bc = \lambda^2 - (a + d)\lambda + (ad - bc)
$$
Recognizing $a + d$ as the trace and $ad - bc$ as the determinant lets us write the $2 \times 2$ characteristic equation in a memorable, universal form:
$$
\lambda^2 - \operatorname{tr}(A)\lambda + \det(A) = 0
$$
Applying the quadratic formula immediately gives both eigenvalues in terms of the trace and determinant:
$$
\lambda = \frac{\operatorname{tr}(A) \pm \sqrt{(\operatorname{tr}(A))^2 - 4\det(A)}}{2}
$$
Notice how easy it is to double-check your work on any $2 \times 2$ problem: once you find $\lambda_1$ and $\lambda_2$, verify immediately that $\lambda_1 + \lambda_2 = a + d$ and $\lambda_1 \lambda_2 = ad - bc$.

## Estimating Eigenvalues: The Gershgorin Circle Theorem

For a $2 \times 2$ or $3 \times 3$ matrix, solving $\det(A - \lambda I) = 0$ is straightforward. What if $A$ is a $1000 \times 1000$ matrix? Expanding a degree-$1000$ determinant is computationally hopeless! Can we quickly estimate *where* the eigenvalues live in the complex plane just by looking at the entries of $A$?

Think about how $A$ is built: if all off-diagonal entries of $A$ were zero, $A$ would be a diagonal matrix and its eigenvalues would sit right at the diagonal entries $a_{11}, a_{22}, \dots, a_{nn}$. When we turn on the off-diagonal entries $a_{ij}$ ($j \ne i$), how far can they push the eigenvalues away from the diagonal entries $a_{ii}$? The **Gershgorin Circle Theorem** gives a beautiful geometric answer: each row $i$ defines a circular disk centered at the diagonal entry $a_{ii}$, with a radius $R_i$ equal to the sum of the absolute values of the off-diagonal entries in that row, and every eigenvalue is trapped inside the union of these disks!

> [!theorem] Gershgorin Circle Theorem
> Every eigenvalue of an $n \times n$ matrix $A$ lies within at least one of the closed disks $D_i$ in the complex plane centered at the diagonal entry $a_{ii}$, with radius equal to the sum of the absolute values of the off-diagonal entries in row $i$:
> $$
> \lambda \in \bigcup_{i=1}^n D_i \quad \text{where} \quad D_i = \left\{ z \in \mathbb{C} : |z - a_{ii}| \le R_i \right\}
> $$
> and the row radius $R_i$ is defined as:
> $$
> R_i = \sum_{\substack{j=1 \\ j \ne i}}^n |a_{ij}|
> $$

**Proof:**
Let $\lambda$ be any eigenvalue of $A$ with a corresponding non-zero eigenvector $x \ne 0$. Writing out $Ax = \lambda x$ row by row gives:
$$
Ax = \lambda x \iff \sum_{j=1}^n a_{ij} x_j = \lambda x_i \quad \text{for all } i = 1, \dots, n
$$
Here is the key trick of the proof: since $x \ne 0$, one of its components must have the largest magnitude. Let $k$ be the index of that largest component:
$$
|x_k| = \max_{1 \le j \le n} |x_j| > 0
$$
Now look specifically at the $k$-th row of the equation $Ax = \lambda x$, and isolate the diagonal term $a_{kk} x_k$ by moving it to the right-hand side:
$$
a_{kk} x_k + \sum_{j \ne k} a_{kj} x_j = \lambda x_k \implies (\lambda - a_{kk}) x_k = \sum_{j \ne k} a_{kj} x_j
$$
Take the absolute value (modulus) of both sides and apply the triangle inequality to the sum on the right:
$$
|\lambda - a_{kk}| |x_k| = \left| \sum_{j \ne k} a_{kj} x_j \right| \le \sum_{j \ne k} |a_{kj}| |x_j|
$$
Because we chose $x_k$ to be the largest component of $x$, every other component satisfies $|x_j| \le |x_k|$. Replacing $|x_j|$ by $|x_k|$ on the right-hand side gives:
$$
|\lambda - a_{kk}| |x_k| \le \left( \sum_{j \ne k} |a_{kj}| \right) |x_k| = R_k |x_k|
$$
Finally, since $|x_k| > 0$, we can divide both sides by $|x_k|$ to obtain:
$$
|\lambda - a_{kk}| \le R_k
$$
This says that the distance in the complex plane from $\lambda$ to the diagonal entry $a_{kk}$ is at most $R_k$—so $\lambda$ lies inside the $k$-th Gershgorin disk $D_k$! Because every eigenvalue has some maximal eigenvector component $k$, every eigenvalue of $A$ must lie in the union $\bigcup_{i=1}^n D_i$. $\blacksquare$

## Special Geometric Matrices

Algebraic formulas are only half the picture. When a matrix represents a familiar geometric transformation—like projecting onto a subspace, reflecting across a mirror, rotating the plane, or stepping through a Markov chain—we can often deduce its eigenvalues and eigenvectors purely from geometry! Let's examine five classic families of matrices.

### Identity Matrix

What does the identity matrix $I$ do to a vector $x \in \mathbb{R}^n$? Nothing at all! Every non-zero vector satisfies:
$$
Ix = 1 \cdot x
$$
Thus, for the identity matrix, *every* non-zero vector in $\mathbb{R}^n$ is an eigenvector, all $n$ eigenvalues equal $\lambda = 1$, and the eigenspace $E_1 = N(I - 1 \cdot I) = \mathbb{R}^n$ is the entire space.

### Projection Matrices

Recall from Chapter 4 that a projection matrix $P$ (satisfying $P^2 = P$) projects every vector in $\mathbb{R}^n$ onto its column space $C(P)$. Which vectors do not change direction when projected by $P$? There are two natural subspaces to check:
- **Vectors already in the subspace $C(P)$:** If a vector $x$ already lies in the column space $C(P)$, projecting it onto $C(P)$ leaves it untouched:
  $$
  Px = x = 1 \cdot x \implies \lambda = 1
  $$
  Thus, the entire column space $C(P)$ is the eigenspace for $\lambda = 1$.
- **Vectors perpendicular to the subspace (in the nullspace $N(P)$):** If a vector $x$ lies in the nullspace $N(P)$, projecting it collapses it straight to the origin:
  $$
  Px = 0 = 0 \cdot x \implies \lambda = 0
  $$
  Thus, the nullspace $N(P)$ is the eigenspace for $\lambda = 0$.

Together, $\dim C(P) = r$ and $\dim N(P) = n - r$ already give us $r + (n - r) = n$ independent eigenvectors! We can also verify algebraically from $P^2 = P$ that no other eigenvalues can possibly exist.

> [!theorem] Spectrum of a Projection Matrix
> The only possible eigenvalues of an idempotent projection matrix $P$ ($P^2 = P$) are $\lambda = 1$ and $\lambda = 0$.

**Proof:**
Suppose $Px = \lambda x$ for some non-zero eigenvector $x \ne 0$. Multiplying both sides on the left by $P$ applies the transformation a second time:
$$
P^2 x = P(Px) = P(\lambda x) = \lambda (Px) = \lambda^2 x
$$
Since $P$ is a projection matrix, $P^2 = P$, which means $P^2 x = Px = \lambda x$. Equating the two results gives:
$$
\lambda^2 x = \lambda x \implies (\lambda^2 - \lambda)x = 0
$$
Because $x \ne 0$ is a non-zero eigenvector, its scalar coefficient must vanish: $\lambda^2 - \lambda = \lambda(\lambda - 1) = 0$. Therefore, $\lambda = 0$ or $\lambda = 1$. $\blacksquare$

### Reflection Matrices

How is a reflection across a mirror subspace related to a projection onto that same subspace? If $Px$ is the projection of $x$ onto the mirror, then the vector from $x$ to the mirror is $Px - x$. To reflect $x$ to the opposite side of the mirror, we go twice as far: $Rx = x + 2(Px - x) = 2Px - x$. In matrix form, every reflection matrix has the structure $R = 2P - I$.

Using our Diagonal Shift and scaling rules (or just checking the geometry), we can read off the eigensystem of $R = 2P - I$ directly from $P$:
- **Vectors lying on the mirror hyperplane ($x \in C(P)$, where $Px = x$):** A vector sitting right on the mirror does not move when reflected:
  $$
  Rx = (2P - I)x = 2Px - x = 2x - x = 1 \cdot x \implies \lambda = 1
  $$
- **Vectors perpendicular to the mirror hyperplane ($x \in N(P)$, where $Px = 0$):** A vector perpendicular to the mirror gets flipped to point in the exact opposite direction:
  $$
  Rx = (2P - I)x = 2(0) - x = -1 \cdot x \implies \lambda = -1
  $$

Notice that $R = 2P - I$ has the **exact same eigenvectors** as $P$, while its eigenvalues are shifted and scaled from $\{1, 0\}$ to $2\{1, 0\} - 1 = \{1, -1\}$!

![[06. Eigenvalues and Eigenvectors-1792032147855.webp]]

### Rotation Matrices and Complex Spectrum

Now let's ask a revealing geometric question: *What are the real eigenvectors of a $2 \times 2$ rotation matrix $Q$ that rotates the entire plane by an angle $\theta$ (where $\theta$ is not a multiple of $\pi$)?*
$$
Q = \begin{bmatrix} \cos\theta & -\sin\theta \\ \sin\theta & \cos\theta \end{bmatrix}
$$
Think about what a rotation by $90^\circ$ (or any angle $\theta \ne k\pi$) does to vectors in $\mathbb{R}^2$: it turns *every single non-zero vector* into a new direction! No real line in the plane stays pointing in the same direction (or opposite direction) after a genuine rotation. Does that mean $Q$ has no eigenvalues?

Let's check the algebra by computing the characteristic equation of $Q$:
$$
\det(Q - \lambda I) = (\cos\theta - \lambda)^2 + \sin^2\theta = \lambda^2 - (2\cos\theta)\lambda + (\cos^2\theta + \sin^2\theta) = \lambda^2 - 2\lambda\cos\theta + 1 = 0
$$
Solving this quadratic equation using $\cos^2\theta - 1 = -\sin^2\theta$ reveals a pair of **complex conjugate eigenvalues** lying on the unit circle in the complex plane:
$$
\lambda = \cos\theta \pm \sqrt{\cos^2\theta - 1} = \cos\theta \pm i\sin\theta = e^{\pm i\theta}
$$
This is a fundamental lesson: **even when a matrix $A$ is $100\%$ real, its eigenvalues and eigenvectors can be complex numbers!** Whenever a real matrix has complex eigenvalues, they always occur in complex conjugate pairs $\lambda$ and $\bar{\lambda}$.

### Markov Matrices and Steady States

Our final family is one of the most important in applied mathematics, probability, and data science. A **Markov matrix** (or **stochastic matrix**) $M$ models transitions between states in a dynamical system (where $m_{ij}$ is the fraction or probability of moving from state $j$ to state $i$ in one time step). A matrix $M$ is a Markov matrix if it satisfies two structural conditions:
1. Every entry is non-negative: $m_{ij} \ge 0$.
2. The entries in each column add up to $1$:
   $$
   \sum_{i=1}^n m_{ij} = 1 \quad \text{for every column } j = 1, \dots, n
   $$

Why does the column-sum condition $\sum_{i=1}^n m_{ij} = 1$ guarantee that a Markov matrix always has a steady state? Because when the columns of $M$ add up to $1$, the rows of $(M - I)$ add up to $0$, making $(M - I)$ singular and forcing $\lambda = 1$ to be an eigenvalue! Let's prove both this fact and the bound $|\lambda_i| \le 1$ on all other eigenvalues.

> [!theorem] Unit Eigenvalue and Spectral Bound of Markov Matrices
> Every Markov matrix $M$ has $\lambda_1 = 1$ as an eigenvalue, and every eigenvalue of $M$ satisfies $|\lambda_i| \le 1$.

**Proof:**
Let $\mathbf{1} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & \dots & 1 \end{bmatrix}^T$ be the all-ones column vector in $\mathbb{R}^n$. When we multiply $M$ on the left by the row vector $\mathbf{1}^T$, the $j$-th entry of the product $\mathbf{1}^T M$ is simply the sum of column $j$ of $M$:
$$
(\mathbf{1}^T M)_j = \sum_{i=1}^n m_{ij} = 1 \implies \mathbf{1}^T M = 1 \cdot \mathbf{1}^T
$$
In other words, $\mathbf{1}^T$ is a **left eigenvector** of $M$ with eigenvalue $\lambda = 1$! Taking the transpose of both sides turns this into a standard right eigenvector equation for $M^T$:
$$
M^T \mathbf{1} = 1 \cdot \mathbf{1}
$$
Since $M$ and $M^T$ have the exact same eigenvalues by the Transpose Spectrum Theorem ($\det(M - \lambda I) = \det(M^T - \lambda I)$), $\lambda_1 = 1$ is guaranteed to be an eigenvalue of $M$.

Next, why must every eigenvalue satisfy $|\lambda| \le 1$? Let's apply the Gershgorin Circle Theorem to $M^T$ (whose rows are the columns of $M$, so its entries are non-negative and each row sums to $1$). For row $j$ of $M^T$, the Gershgorin disk is centered at the non-negative diagonal entry $m_{jj} \ge 0$, and its radius is the sum of the remaining non-negative entries in column $j$ of $M$: $R_j = \sum_{i \ne j} m_{ij} = 1 - m_{jj}$. Thus, every eigenvalue $\lambda$ of $M$ (and $M^T$) satisfies:
$$
|\lambda - m_{jj}| \le 1 - m_{jj}
$$
By the triangle inequality, the modulus of $\lambda = (\lambda - m_{jj}) + m_{jj}$ is bounded by:
$$
|\lambda| \le |\lambda - m_{jj}| + |m_{jj}| \le (1 - m_{jj}) + m_{jj} = 1
$$
Therefore, no eigenvalue of a Markov matrix can ever lie outside the unit circle $|\lambda| \le 1$. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Steady-State Distribution and Long-Term Convergence
> While $\mathbf{1}$ is the eigenvector of $M^T$ for $\lambda_1 = 1$, the corresponding right eigenvector $x_1$ of $M$ itself satisfies:
> $$
> M x_1 = x_1
> $$
> This vector $x_1$ represents the **steady-state distribution** (stationary distribution) of the Markov chain: once the system reaches $x_1$, stepping forward in time leaves the state unchanged. Moreover, if we decompose any initial probability distribution $v$ into eigenvectors as $v = c_1 x_1 + \sum_{i=2}^n c_i x_i$, then after $k$ steps:
> $$
> M^k v = c_1 (1)^k x_1 + \sum_{i=2}^n c_i \lambda_i^k x_i \xrightarrow{k \to \infty} c_1 x_1 \quad (\text{provided } |\lambda_i| < 1 \text{ for all } i \ge 2)
> $$
> Because all modes with $|\lambda_i| < 1$ decay to zero as $\lambda_i^k \to 0$, the system forgets its initial state and converges to the steady-state eigenvector $x_1$!

To wrap up our tour of special geometric matrices, the table below summarizes the key matrix families we will encounter throughout this chapter, along with the behavior of their eigenvalues and eigenvectors:

| Matrix Family | Algebraic Definition | Nature of Eigenvalues $\lambda$ | Nature of Eigenvectors $x$ |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Symmetric Matrix** | $S^T = S$ | Purely real: $\lambda \in \mathbb{R}$ | Mutually orthogonal |
| **Skew-Symmetric Matrix** | $A^T = -A$ | Purely imaginary: $\lambda = ib$ ($b \in \mathbb{R}$) | Mutually orthogonal |
| **Orthogonal Matrix** | $Q^T Q = I$ | Unit magnitude: $\lvert\lambda\rvert = 1$ ($e^{i\theta}$) | Mutually orthogonal |
| **Markov Matrix** | $M_{ij} \ge 0, \sum_{i} M_{ij} = 1$ | $\lambda_{\max} = 1$, all $\lvert\lambda_i\rvert \le 1$ | Steady-state eigenvector $x_1 \ge 0$ |
| **Projection Matrix** | $P^2 = P, P^T = P$ | $\lambda \in \{0, 1\}$ | Orthogonal subspaces ($C(P) \perp N(P)$) |

> [!note] Analogy with the Complex Plane
> Look at the first three rows of the table above—there is a beautiful analogy between these three fundamental matrix families and subsets of complex numbers $z \in \mathbb{C}$ (where matrix transposition $A^T$ acts like complex conjugation $\bar{z}$):
> - **Symmetric matrices ($S^T = S$)** mirror real numbers ($\bar{z} = z$), and their eigenvalues lie purely on the **real line** ($\operatorname{Re}(z)$).
> - **Skew-symmetric matrices ($A^T = -A$)** mirror purely imaginary numbers ($\bar{z} = -z$), and their eigenvalues lie purely on the **imaginary axis** ($\operatorname{Im}(z)$).
> - **Orthogonal matrices ($Q^T Q = I$)** mirror complex numbers of unit modulus ($\bar{z} z = |z|^2 = 1$), and their eigenvalues lie on the **unit circle** ($|z| = 1$).

## Problem Set 6.1

### Exercise 11: Columns of $A - \lambda_1 I$ as Multiples of $x_2$

**Problem Statement:**
Here is a strange fact about $2 \times 2$ matrices with eigenvalues $\lambda_1 \ne \lambda_2$: The columns of $A - \lambda_1 I$ are multiples of the eigenvector $x_2$. Any idea why this should be?

**Solution:**
At first glance, this sounds almost backwards—we usually look at the **nullspace** of $A - \lambda_1 I$ to find $x_1$, so why does the **column space** of $A - \lambda_1 I$ give us the *other* eigenvector $x_2$?

Let's see what happens when we multiply the second eigenvector $x_2 \ne 0$ (which satisfies $A x_2 = \lambda_2 x_2$) by the shifted matrix $(A - \lambda_1 I)$:
$$
(A - \lambda_1 I)x_2 = A x_2 - \lambda_1 x_2 = \lambda_2 x_2 - \lambda_1 x_2 = (\lambda_2 - \lambda_1)x_2
$$
Because the two eigenvalues are distinct ($\lambda_1 \ne \lambda_2$), the scalar factor $(\lambda_2 - \lambda_1)$ is non-zero! Dividing both sides by $(\lambda_2 - \lambda_1)$ gives:
$$
x_2 = \frac{1}{\lambda_2 - \lambda_1}(A - \lambda_1 I)x_2
$$
Look at what the right-hand side is: $(A - \lambda_1 I)$ times a vector is a linear combination of the columns of $(A - \lambda_1 I)$. That proves $x_2$ lies inside the column space $C(A - \lambda_1 I)$!

Now, how big is the column space $C(A - \lambda_1 I)$ for this $2 \times 2$ matrix?
1. Because $\lambda_1$ is an eigenvalue, $(A - \lambda_1 I)$ is singular ($\det(A - \lambda_1 I) = 0$), so its rank is strictly less than $2$: $\operatorname{rank}(A - \lambda_1 I) < 2$.
2. Because $\lambda_1 \ne \lambda_2$, the matrix $A - \lambda_1 I$ cannot be the zero matrix (if $A - \lambda_1 I = 0$, then $A = \lambda_1 I$, which would have $\lambda_1$ as a repeated eigenvalue and contradict $\lambda_1 \ne \lambda_2$). Thus, $\operatorname{rank}(A - \lambda_1 I) = 1$.

Since $C(A - \lambda_1 I)$ is a $1$-dimensional line in $\mathbb{R}^2$ and the non-zero vector $x_2$ lies on that line, the entire column space is simply the line spanned by $x_2$:
$$
C(A - \lambda_1 I) = \operatorname{span}\{x_2\}
$$
Every column of $A - \lambda_1 I$ belongs to its column space, so every column of $A - \lambda_1 I$ must be a scalar multiple of $x_2$! *(And by the exact same logic, every column of $A - \lambda_2 I$ is a scalar multiple of $x_1$, giving a great shortcut for reading off $2 \times 2$ eigenvectors.)*

### Exercise 19: Deducing Spectral Information from Given Eigenvalues

**Problem Statement:**
A $3 \times 3$ matrix $B$ is known to have eigenvalues $0, 1, 2$. This information is enough to find three of these (give the answers where possible):
1. The rank of $B$
2. The determinant of $B^T B$
3. The eigenvalues of $B^T B$
4. The eigenvalues of $(B^2 + I)^{-1}$.

**Solution:**
Let's test which of these four quantities depend *only* on the eigenvalues of $B$, and which one depends on the eigenvectors too:

1. **The rank of $B$:**
   We are told that the $3 \times 3$ matrix $B$ has three distinct eigenvalues: $\lambda_1 = 0$, $\lambda_2 = 1$, and $\lambda_3 = 2$. Because $\lambda_1 = 0$ is a non-repeated root of the characteristic polynomial (algebraic multiplicity $1$), the nullspace $N(B) = N(B - 0 \cdot I)$ has dimension exactly $1$:
   $$
   \dim N(B) = 1
   $$
   By the Rank-Nullity Theorem, the rank of $B$ is:
   $$
   \operatorname{rank}(B) = n - \dim N(B) = 3 - 1 = 2
   $$

2. **The determinant of $B^T B$:**
   Since the determinant of any matrix is the product of its eigenvalues, $\det(B)$ is immediately zero:
   $$
   \det(B) = \lambda_1 \lambda_2 \lambda_3 = 0 \cdot 1 \cdot 2 = 0
   $$
   Using the product rule for determinants together with $\det(B^T) = \det(B)$, we get:
   $$
   \det(B^T B) = \det(B^T)\det(B) = (\det(B))^2 = 0^2 = 0
   $$

3. **The eigenvalues of $B^T B$:**
   **Cannot be determined from the eigenvalues of $B$ alone!**
   Why not? As we saw earlier, $B$ and $B^T$ share the same eigenvalues, but they do *not* generally share the same eigenvectors. Because $B$ and $B^T$ can have completely different eigenvectors, the eigenvalues of their product $B^T B$ are **not** simply $\lambda_i^2$ (unless $B$ happens to be symmetric or normal). In fact, the eigenvalues of $B^T B$ are the squared singular values $\sigma_i^2$, which depend on the lengths and angles of the eigenvectors of $B$. (The only one we know for sure is that $\lambda = 0$ is still an eigenvalue of $B^T B$ since $\det(B^T B) = 0$.)

4. **The eigenvalues of $(B^2 + I)^{-1}$:**
   Unlike $B^T B$, the matrix $(B^2 + I)^{-1}$ is built purely from powers and identity shifts of $B$ itself, so every eigenvector $x$ of $B$ (satisfying $Bx = \lambda x$) is automatically an eigenvector of $(B^2 + I)^{-1}$! First, applying $B^2 + I$ to $x$ gives:
   $$
   (B^2 + I)x = B^2 x + Ix = \lambda^2 x + x = (\lambda^2 + 1)x
   $$
   Since $\lambda^2 + 1 \ne 0$ for each of $\lambda \in \{0, 1, 2\}$, $(B^2 + I)$ is invertible, and by our inverse rule (Property 2), the eigenvalues of $(B^2 + I)^{-1}$ are simply the reciprocals:
   $$
   (B^2 + I)^{-1} x = \frac{1}{\lambda^2 + 1} x
   $$
   Plugging in $\lambda = 0, 1, 2$ one by one gives the three eigenvalues of $(B^2 + I)^{-1}$:
   - For $\lambda_1 = 0$: $\frac{1}{0^2 + 1} = 1$
   - For $\lambda_2 = 1$: $\frac{1}{1^2 + 1} = \frac{1}{2}$
   - For $\lambda_3 = 2$: $\frac{1}{2^2 + 1} = \frac{1}{5}$

   Thus, the eigenvalues of $(B^2 + I)^{-1}$ are $1, \frac{1}{2}, \frac{1}{5}$.

### Exercise 26: Spectrum of Block Triangular Matrices

**Problem Statement:**
The block $B$ has eigenvalues $1, 2$ and $C$ has eigenvalues $3, 4$ and $D$ has eigenvalues $5, 7$. Find the eigenvalues of the $4 \times 4$ matrix $A$:
$$
A = \begin{bmatrix} B & C \\ 0 & D \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 & 3 & 0 \\ -2 & 3 & 0 & 4 \\ 0 & 0 & 6 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 & 1 & 6 \end{bmatrix}
$$

**Solution:**
Just as the eigenvalues of an ordinary upper triangular matrix sit on its main diagonal (completely independent of the entries above the diagonal), what happens when a matrix is **block upper triangular**?

When we subtract $\lambda I_4$ from $A$, the shifted matrix $A - \lambda I$ preserves the exact same $2 \times 2$ block upper triangular structure, with $0$ in the lower-left block:
$$
A - \lambda I = \begin{bmatrix} B - \lambda I & C \\ 0 & D - \lambda I \end{bmatrix}
$$
Recall from Chapter 5 that for any block triangular matrix with square diagonal blocks, the determinant is simply the product of the determinants of the diagonal blocks:
$$
\det(A - \lambda I) = \det(B - \lambda I) \det(D - \lambda I)
$$
Notice that the upper-right block $C$ completely drops out of the determinant! Setting $\det(A - \lambda I) = 0$ tells us that the characteristic polynomial of $A$ is just the product of the characteristic polynomials of $B$ and $D$:
$$
p_A(\lambda) = p_B(\lambda) p_D(\lambda) = 0
$$
Therefore, the eigenvalues of $A$ are simply the eigenvalues of $B$ combined with the eigenvalues of $D$ (and the eigenvalues of $C$ are a complete red herring):
- For block $B = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ -2 & 3 \end{bmatrix}$, we have $\operatorname{tr}(B) = 3$ and $\det(B) = 2$:
  $$
  \det(B - \lambda I) = \lambda^2 - 3\lambda + 2 = (\lambda - 1)(\lambda - 2) = 0 \implies \lambda \in \{1, 2\}
  $$
- For block $D = \begin{bmatrix} 6 & 1 \\ 1 & 6 \end{bmatrix}$, we have $\operatorname{tr}(D) = 12$ and $\det(D) = 35$:
  $$
  \det(D - \lambda I) = (6 - \lambda)^2 - 1 = \lambda^2 - 12\lambda + 35 = (\lambda - 5)(\lambda - 7) = 0 \implies \lambda \in \{5, 7\}
  $$

Combining the two sets gives the four eigenvalues of $A$:
$$
\lambda = 1, \, 2, \, 5, \, 7
$$

### Challenge Problems

#### Problem 35: Determinants, Pivots, Traces, and Spectrum of $3 \times 3$ Permutations

**Problem Statement:**
There are six $3 \times 3$ permutation matrices $P$. What numbers can be the *determinants* of $P$? What numbers can be *pivots*? What numbers can be the *trace* of $P$? What *four numbers* can be eigenvalues of $P$, as in Problem 15?

**Solution:**
To organize our thinking across all $3! = 6$ permutation matrices of size $3 \times 3$, let's group them into three natural families based on how many rows they swap (their cycle structure):
- **The Identity ($1$ matrix):** $I = \operatorname{diag}(1, 1, 1)$ requires $0$ row exchanges and leaves all $3$ rows fixed in place.
- **Single Row Swaps / Transpositions ($3$ matrices):** Each swaps two rows and leaves $1$ row fixed in place ($1$ row exchange):
  $$
  P_{12} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}, \quad P_{13} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 1 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}, \quad P_{23} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 1 & 0 \end{bmatrix}
  $$
- **Cyclic Shifts / $3$-Cycles ($2$ matrices):** Each cycles all three rows so that $0$ rows stay in their original positions (requiring $2$ row exchanges to return to $I$):
  $$
  P_{123} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}, \quad P_{132} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & 0 \end{bmatrix}
  $$

Now let's answer each of the four questions systematically:

1. **Determinants:**
   Every row exchange flips the sign of the determinant from $\det(I) = +1$:
   - Even permutations ($I$ and the two $3$-cycles, requiring $0$ or $2$ swaps) have $\det(P) = +1$.
   - Odd permutations (the three single-swap transpositions, requiring $1$ swap) have $\det(P) = -1$.
   $$
   \det(P) \in \{1, -1\}
   $$

2. **Pivots:**
   Every permutation matrix already has a single $1$ in each row and column and zeros everywhere else. During Gaussian elimination, row exchanges immediately reorder $P$ back into the identity matrix $I = \operatorname{diag}(1, 1, 1)$, so every single pivot of any permutation matrix is $1$:
   $$
   \text{Pivots} \in \{1\}
   $$

3. **Trace:**
   The trace $\operatorname{tr}(P) = \sum_{i=1}^3 p_{ii}$ simply counts how many $1$s sit on the main diagonal—which is the number of rows that remain fixed in their original position! Notice why $\operatorname{tr}(P) = 2$ is impossible: if $2$ rows are in their right places, the $3\text{rd}$ row has nowhere else to go and must be in its right place too.
   - For $I$: all $3$ ones sit on the diagonal $\implies \operatorname{tr}(P) = 3$.
   - For the three transpositions: $1$ one sits on the diagonal $\implies \operatorname{tr}(P) = 1$.
   - For the two $3$-cycles: $0$ ones sit on the diagonal $\implies \operatorname{tr}(P) = 0$.
   $$
   \operatorname{tr}(P) \in \{0, 1, 3\}
   $$

4. **Four distinct numbers that can be eigenvalues of $P$:**
   - **For $I$:** All eigenvalues are $\lambda = 1$ (with multiplicity $3$).
   - **For the three transpositions:** Swapping two rows twice brings every vector back to where it started ($P^2 = I$), so $\lambda^2 = 1 \implies \lambda = \pm 1$. Since $\operatorname{tr}(P) = 1$ and $\det(P) = -1$, the three eigenvalues are $1, 1, -1$:
     $$
     \det(P - \lambda I) = (1 - \lambda)(\lambda^2 - 1) = (1 - \lambda)^2 (1 + \lambda) = 0 \implies \lambda \in \{1, -1\}
     $$
   - **For the two $3$-cycles:** Cycling three rows three times brings every vector back to the start ($P^3 = I$), so the eigenvalues are the three cube roots of unity ($\lambda^3 = 1$):
     $$
     \det(P - \lambda I) = 1 - \lambda^3 = (1 - \lambda)(1 + \lambda + \lambda^2) = 0 \implies \lambda \in \left\{ 1, \; e^{2\pi i/3}, \; e^{-2\pi i/3} \right\}
     $$
   Collecting all distinct values across the six matrices gives four possible eigenvalues (all lying on the unit circle $|\lambda| = 1$):
   $$
   \lambda \in \left\{ 1, \; -1, \; e^{2\pi i/3}, \; e^{-2\pi i/3} \right\} = \left\{ 1, \; -1, \; -\frac{1}{2} + i\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, \; -\frac{1}{2} - i\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \right\}
   $$

#### Problem 36: Derivation of the Heisenberg Uncertainty Inequality

**Problem Statement:**
(Heisenberg's Uncertainty Principle) $AB - BA = I$ can happen for infinite matrices with $A = A^T$ and $B = -B^T$. Then
$$
x^T x = x^T AB x - x^T BA x \le 2 \|Ax\| \|Bx\|
$$
Explain that last step by using the Schwarz inequality $|u^T v| \le \|u\| \|v\|$. Then Heisenberg's inequality says that $\|Ax\| / \|x\|$ times $\|Bx\| / \|x\|$ is at least $\frac{1}{2}$. It is impossible to get the position error and momentum error both very small.

**Solution:**
Before diving into the inequality, why does the problem statement carefully note that $AB - BA = I$ can happen for **infinite matrices**? Because for finite $n \times n$ matrices, our trace rules from earlier make $AB - BA = I$ completely impossible! By linearity and cyclic invariance ($\operatorname{tr}(AB) = \operatorname{tr}(BA)$), the trace of the left-hand side is always $\operatorname{tr}(AB - BA) = \operatorname{tr}(AB) - \operatorname{tr}(BA) = 0$, whereas the trace of the right-hand side is $\operatorname{tr}(I_n) = n \ne 0$. In infinite dimensions—such as quantum-mechanical differential operators acting on functions $f(t)$, where $Af(t) = t f(t)$ (position) and $Bf(t) = \frac{d}{dt}f(t)$ (momentum)—the product rule gives $(BA - AB)f(t) = \frac{d}{dt}(t f(t)) - t f'(t) = f(t)$, so the commutator equals the identity operator $I$!

Now let's derive the uncertainty inequality step by step:

1. **Derivation of the identity and Cauchy-Schwarz bound:**
   Starting from $AB - BA = I$, multiply on the left by $x^T$ and on the right by $x$:
   $$
   x^T (AB - BA) x = x^T I x \implies x^T AB x - x^T BA x = x^T x = \|x\|^2
   $$
   Next, let's rewrite both terms on the left-hand side in terms of the vectors $Ax$ and $Bx$ by using the symmetry of $A$ ($A^T = A$) and the skew-symmetry of $B$ ($B^T = -B \iff -B = B^T$):
   - **First term:** Since $A = A^T$, we can write $x^T A = (A^T x)^T = (Ax)^T$:
     $$
     x^T AB x = (A^T x)^T (Bx) = (Ax)^T (Bx)
     $$
   - **Second term:** Using $-B = B^T$, we have $x^T (-B) = x^T B^T = (Bx)^T$. Since the dot product of two real vectors is symmetric, $(Bx)^T (Ax) = (Ax)^T (Bx)$:
     $$
     -x^T BA x = x^T (-B) Ax = x^T B^T Ax = (Bx)^T (Ax) = (Ax)^T (Bx)
     $$
   Notice that the two terms do not cancel—they **add together** to give twice the inner product of $u = Ax$ and $v = Bx$:
   $$
   x^T x = (Ax)^T (Bx) + (Ax)^T (Bx) = 2 (Ax)^T (Bx)
   $$
   Taking the absolute value of both sides and applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality $|u^T v| \le \|u\| \|v\|$ with $u = Ax$ and $v = Bx$ immediately produces the upper bound:
   $$
   \|x\|^2 = |x^T x| = 2 |(Ax)^T (Bx)| \le 2 \|Ax\| \|Bx\|
   $$

2. **Heisenberg's Uncertainty Inequality:**
   For any non-zero state vector $x \ne 0$, divide both sides of $\|x\|^2 \le 2 \|Ax\| \|Bx\|$ by $2 \|x\|^2$:
   $$
   \frac{\|Ax\|}{\|x\|} \frac{\|Bx\|}{\|x\|} \ge \frac{1}{2}
   $$
   In quantum mechanics, the normalized ratio $\Delta x = \frac{\|Ax\|}{\|x\|}$ measures the position uncertainty (error) and $\Delta p = \frac{\|Bx\|}{\|x\|}$ measures the momentum uncertainty of a particle in state $x$. Because their product is bounded below by $\frac{1}{2}$:
   $$
   \Delta x \cdot \Delta p \ge \frac{1}{2}
   $$
   squeezing the position uncertainty $\Delta x$ to be extremely small forces the momentum uncertainty $\Delta p$ to blow up! Because the position and momentum operators do not commute ($AB - BA = I$), they cannot share simultaneous eigenvectors, making it fundamentally impossible to localize both position and momentum with arbitrary precision at the same time.

---

# 6.2 Diagonalizing a Matrix

In Chapter 2, our workhorse for solving linear systems $Ax = b$ was elimination, which factors a matrix into lower and upper triangular pieces ($A = LU$). But when we study linear dynamical systems, difference equations, or high matrix powers $A^k$, triangular factors are no longer the natural language—multiplying $LU$ by itself a hundred times is a mess! Instead, the eigenvalue problem gives us a completely different kind of factorization. Whenever an $n \times n$ matrix $A$ has a full set of $n$ linearly independent eigenvectors, we can factor it into its eigenvectors and eigenvalues:
$$
A = X\Lambda X^{-1}
$$
Why is this factorization so powerful? Because the eigenvector matrix $X$ acts as a natural coordinate transformation that completely uncouples the system, turning the complicated matrix operator $A$ into a pure diagonal scaling matrix $\Lambda$.

## The Diagonalization Theorem: $A = X\Lambda X^{-1}$

### Matrix Formulation of the Eigenvalue Problem

Suppose $A$ is an $n \times n$ square matrix that possesses $n$ linearly independent eigenvectors $x_1, x_2, \dots, x_n$ with corresponding eigenvalues $\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \dots, \lambda_n$. Instead of writing out $n$ separate vector equations
$$
A x_j = \lambda_j x_j \quad (j = 1, 2, \dots, n)
$$
how can we pack all $n$ eigenvalue equations into a single, compact matrix equation?

We start by assembling the **eigenvector matrix** $X$, placing the eigenvectors $x_1, \dots, x_n$ side by side as its columns, and the **eigenvalue matrix** $\Lambda$, placing the eigenvalues $\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_n$ along its main diagonal:
$$
X = \begin{bmatrix} x_1 & x_2 & \cdots & x_n \end{bmatrix}, \quad \Lambda = \begin{bmatrix} \lambda_1 & & & \\ & \lambda_2 & & \\ & & \ddots & \\ & & & \lambda_n \end{bmatrix} = \operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \dots, \lambda_n)
$$
Now let's multiply $A$ by the eigenvector matrix $X$. Because matrix multiplication acts on one column of $X$ at a time, each column $x_j$ simply gets scaled by its own eigenvalue $\lambda_j$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
AX &= A \begin{bmatrix} x_1 & x_2 & \cdots & x_n \end{bmatrix} \\
   &= \begin{bmatrix} Ax_1 & Ax_2 & \cdots & Ax_n \end{bmatrix} \\
   &= \begin{bmatrix} \lambda_1 x_1 & \lambda_2 x_2 & \cdots & \lambda_n x_n \end{bmatrix}
\end{aligned}
$$
How do we write that final matrix $\begin{bmatrix} \lambda_1 x_1 & \lambda_2 x_2 & \cdots & \lambda_n x_n \end{bmatrix}$ as a product of $X$ and $\Lambda$? *Why must $\Lambda$ sit on the right of $X$ in $AX = X\Lambda$ rather than on the left?*

Think about how block and column multiplication work: when you multiply $X$ on the **right** by a diagonal matrix $\Lambda$, the $j$-th column of $X\Lambda$ is a linear combination of the columns of $X$ weighted by the $j$-th column of $\Lambda$—which has $\lambda_j$ in position $j$ and zeros everywhere else! Thus, right-multiplying by $\Lambda$ scales each column $j$ of $X$ by $\lambda_j$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
X \Lambda &= \begin{bmatrix} x_1 & x_2 & \cdots & x_n \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \lambda_1 & & & \\ & \lambda_2 & & \\ & & \ddots & \\ & & & \lambda_n \end{bmatrix} \\
          &= \begin{bmatrix} \lambda_1 x_1 & \lambda_2 x_2 & \cdots & \lambda_n x_n \end{bmatrix}
\end{aligned}
$$
Equating these two expressions gives us the fundamental matrix form of the eigenvalue problem:
$$
AX = X\Lambda
$$

> [!warning] Right vs. Left Multiplication by a Diagonal Matrix
> Keep a close eye on the order of multiplication whenever a diagonal matrix is involved:
> - **Post-multiplication ($X\Lambda$)** multiplies the **columns** of $X$ by the diagonal entries $\lambda_j$.
> - **Pre-multiplication ($\Lambda X$)** multiplies the **rows** of $X$ by the diagonal entries $\lambda_i$.
> 
> If we accidentally wrote $AX = \Lambda X$, we would be scaling the *rows* of $X$ instead of its *columns*! Writing $AX = X\Lambda$ ensures that each column transformation matches $Ax_j = \lambda_j x_j$.

### Inversion and the Diagonalization Factorization

Notice that the equation $AX = X\Lambda$ holds for any collection of eigenvectors we place into the columns of $X$. However, to turn $AX = X\Lambda$ into a true factorization of $A$, we need to divide by $X$—which means $X$ must be **invertible**! When is the square matrix $X$ invertible? Precisely when its $n$ column vectors $x_1, \dots, x_n$ are linearly independent (giving $X$ full rank $n$).

> [!theorem] The Diagonalization Theorem
> An $n \times n$ matrix $A$ is **diagonalizable** if and only if it possesses $n$ linearly independent eigenvectors. When $X$ has those $n$ independent eigenvectors as its columns and $\Lambda = \operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_n)$ is the diagonal matrix of corresponding eigenvalues, $X$ is invertible and:
> $$
> X^{-1} A X = \Lambda \iff A = X\Lambda X^{-1}
> $$

**Proof:**
Let's verify both directions of this equivalence to see why having $n$ independent eigenvectors is both sufficient and necessary:

1. **Sufficiency ($n$ independent eigenvectors $\implies A = X\Lambda X^{-1}$):**
   Suppose $A$ has $n$ linearly independent eigenvectors $x_1, \dots, x_n$. Placing them into the columns of $X = \begin{bmatrix} x_1 & \dots & x_n \end{bmatrix}$ produces a square matrix of full rank $n$, so $X$ is non-singular and $X^{-1}$ exists. Since each column satisfies $Ax_j = \lambda_j x_j$, we have $AX = X\Lambda$. Multiplying $AX = X\Lambda$ on the left by $X^{-1}$ diagonalizes $A$:
   $$
   X^{-1} A X = X^{-1} X \Lambda = I \Lambda = \Lambda
   $$
   Multiplying $AX = X\Lambda$ on the right by $X^{-1}$ recovers the factorization of $A$:
   $$
   A = X\Lambda X^{-1}
   $$

2. **Necessity ($A = X\Lambda X^{-1} \implies n$ independent eigenvectors):**
   Conversely, suppose there exists some invertible matrix $X = \begin{bmatrix} x_1 & \dots & x_n \end{bmatrix}$ and a diagonal matrix $\Lambda = \operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_n)$ such that $A = X\Lambda X^{-1}$. Multiplying both sides on the right by $X$ gives:
   $$
   AX = X\Lambda \implies \begin{bmatrix} Ax_1 & \dots & Ax_n \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \lambda_1 x_1 & \dots & \lambda_n x_n \end{bmatrix}
   $$
   Comparing the two matrices column by column forces $Ax_j = \lambda_j x_j$ for each $j = 1, \dots, n$. Because $X$ is invertible, its columns $x_1, \dots, x_n$ must be non-zero and linearly independent. Thus, the columns of $X$ are $n$ linearly independent eigenvectors of $A$, and the diagonal entries of $\Lambda$ are the corresponding eigenvalues. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Non-Uniqueness, Ordering, and Left Eigenvectors
> While the eigenvalues of $A$ are fixed by $\det(A - \lambda I) = 0$, the factorization $A = X\Lambda X^{-1}$ is **not unique** in two natural ways, and it simultaneously encodes the left eigenvectors of $A$:
> 1. **Eigenvector Scaling:** Any non-zero scalar multiple of an eigenvector is still an eigenvector along the same line ($A(c_j x_j) = \lambda_j (c_j x_j)$). If we scale the columns of $X$ by replacing $X$ with $X D$, where $D = \operatorname{diag}(c_1, \dots, c_n)$ is any invertible diagonal matrix, the scaling factors $D$ and $D^{-1}$ cancel out across the diagonal matrix $\Lambda$:
>    $$
>    (XD)\Lambda (XD)^{-1} = X D \Lambda D^{-1} X^{-1} = X \Lambda (D D^{-1}) X^{-1} = X\Lambda X^{-1} = A
>    $$
>    In numerical software, it is standard practice to fix this scaling freedom by normalizing each column to unit length ($\|x_j\| = 1$).
> 2. **Ordering Correspondence:** There is no sacred order for the eigenvalues, as long as the diagonal entries of $\Lambda$ appear in the **exact same sequential order** as the column eigenvectors in $X$. If you permute the columns of $X$ using a permutation matrix $P$, you must apply the matching permutation to the diagonal of $\Lambda$:
>    $$
>    \begin{alignat*}{2}
>    X_{\text{new}}      &= XP &\quad& \implies X = X_{\text{new}}P^{T} \\[2mm]
>    X_{\text{new}}^{-1} &= P^{T}X^{-1} &\quad& \implies X^{-1} = PX_{\text{new}}^{-1} \\[2mm]
>    A &= X\Lambda X^{-1} &\quad& \implies A = X_{\text{new}}\,(P^{T}\Lambda P)\,X_{\text{new}}^{-1} \\[2mm]
>    \Lambda_{\text{new}} &= P^{-1}\Lambda P = P^{T}\Lambda P &\quad&
>    \end{alignat*}
>    $$
> 3. **Left Eigenvectors in $X^{-1}$:** What about the inverse matrix $X^{-1}$? While the *columns* of $X$ are the right eigenvectors of $A$, multiplying $A = X\Lambda X^{-1}$ on the left by $X^{-1}$ shows that $X^{-1} A = \Lambda X^{-1}$. Reading this equation row by row reveals that the **rows** $y_i^T$ of $X^{-1}$ are the **left eigenvectors** of $A$ ($y_i^T A = \lambda_i y_i^T$), which we will unpack in detail in `### Spectral Decomposition (Dyadic Expansion)` below.

## Linear Independence of Eigenvectors for Distinct Eigenvalues

The Diagonalization Theorem tells us that $A$ is diagonalizable whenever it has $n$ linearly independent eigenvectors. That immediately raises a practical question: *When are we guaranteed that the eigenvectors of $A$ are linearly independent without having to assemble $X$ and check its rank by elimination?*

Remarkably, if eigenvalues are **distinct** ($\lambda_i \ne \lambda_j$), their eigenvectors can never depend on one another—they are automatically linearly independent!

> [!theorem] Linear Independence of Eigenvectors from Distinct Eigenvalues
> Let $\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \dots, \lambda_k$ be distinct eigenvalues of an $n \times n$ matrix $A$ ($\lambda_i \ne \lambda_j$ for all $i \ne j$), with corresponding non-zero eigenvectors $x_1, x_2, \dots, x_k$. Then the set of eigenvectors $\{x_1, x_2, \dots, x_k\}$ is linearly independent.

**Proof (by Annihilation Operator):**
Suppose a linear combination of these $k$ eigenvectors adds up to the zero vector:
$$
c_1 x_1 + c_2 x_2 + \dots + c_k x_k = 0
$$
We want to show that every coefficient $c_1, \dots, c_k$ must be zero. *How can we isolate a single eigenvector coefficient $c_m$ from this sum to prove linear independence?*

Think about what the shifted matrix $(A - \lambda_j I)$ does to any eigenvector $x_i$:
$$
(A - \lambda_j I)x_i = A x_i - \lambda_j x_i = (\lambda_i - \lambda_j)x_i
$$
Notice that if $i = j$, $(A - \lambda_j I)x_j = (\lambda_j - \lambda_j)x_j = 0$—the shifted matrix $(A - \lambda_j I)$ completely **annihilates** $x_j$, while merely multiplying every other eigenvector $x_i$ ($i \ne j$) by the non-zero scalar $(\lambda_i - \lambda_j)$!

So, to isolate a specific coefficient $c_m$ and wipe out all the other eigenvectors $x_j$ ($j \ne m$) in one stroke, we construct the polynomial **annihilation operator** $T_m$ by multiplying together all the shifted matrices except $(A - \lambda_m I)$:
$$
T_m = \prod_{\substack{j=1 \\ j \ne m}}^k (A - \lambda_j I)
$$
Because all the shifted matrices $(A - \lambda_j I)$ are polynomials in $A$, they commute with one another. When we apply $T_m$ to any eigenvector $x_i$, each factor $(A - \lambda_j I)$ contributes a scalar factor $(\lambda_i - \lambda_j)$:
$$
T_m x_i = \left( \prod_{\substack{j=1 \\ j \ne m}}^k (\lambda_i - \lambda_j) \right) x_i
$$
Let's check what happens to this product for different indices $i$:
- **When $i \ne m$:** The index $j = i$ is included in the product, so one of the factors is $(\lambda_i - \lambda_i) = 0$. Therefore, $T_m x_i = 0$ for every $i \ne m$!
- **When $i = m$:** The index $j = m$ is excluded from the product. Because all eigenvalues are distinct, every single factor $(\lambda_m - \lambda_j)$ is non-zero, and since $x_m \ne 0$:
  $$
  T_m x_m = \left( \prod_{\substack{j=1 \\ j \ne m}}^k (\lambda_m - \lambda_j) \right) x_m \ne 0
  $$

Now let's apply our operator $T_m$ to both sides of the original relation $\sum_{i=1}^k c_i x_i = 0$:
$$
T_m \left( \sum_{i=1}^k c_i x_i \right) = \sum_{i=1}^k c_i T_m x_i = c_m \left( \prod_{\substack{j=1 \\ j \ne m}}^k (\lambda_m - \lambda_j) \right) x_m = 0
$$
Every term with $i \ne m$ has vanished! Since the scalar product $\prod_{j \ne m} (\lambda_m - \lambda_j)$ is non-zero and the eigenvector $x_m$ is non-zero, the only way this expression can equal the zero vector is if:
$$
c_m = 0
$$
Repeating this exact annihilation argument for each $m = 1, 2, \dots, k$ forces $c_1 = c_2 = \dots = c_k = 0$, proving that $\{x_1, x_2, \dots, x_k\}$ is linearly independent. $\blacksquare$

> [!summary] Sufficient Condition for Diagonalizability
> If an $n \times n$ matrix $A$ has **$n$ distinct eigenvalues** in $\mathbb{C}$, then its $n$ corresponding eigenvectors are automatically linearly independent. Consequently, the eigenvector matrix $X$ is guaranteed to be invertible, and $A$ is guaranteed to be diagonalizable—no rank checks needed!

## Conditions for Diagonalizability: Algebraic vs. Geometric Multiplicity

Wait: having $n$ distinct eigenvalues is a *sufficient* condition for diagonalizability, but is it *necessary*? What happens when a matrix has **repeated eigenvalues**?

Let's pause and test our intuition with two extreme $2 \times 2$ examples:
- Take the identity matrix $I = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}$. Its characteristic polynomial is $(1 - \lambda)^2 = 0$, so $\lambda = 1$ is repeated twice. Yet $I$ is already diagonal! Every non-zero vector in $\mathbb{R}^2$ is an eigenvector of $I$, so we can easily pick two independent eigenvectors.
- Now take the upper-triangular matrix $N = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}$. Its characteristic polynomial is $\lambda^2 = 0$, so $\lambda = 0$ is also repeated twice. But when we solve $Nx = 0x$, only vectors along the single line $\begin{bmatrix} c \\ 0 \end{bmatrix}$ survive; there is no second independent eigenvector anywhere in $\mathbb{R}^2$!

Why does $\lambda = 1$ give two independent eigenvectors for $I$, whereas $\lambda = 0$ gives only one eigenvector for $N$? Because every eigenvalue actually has **two different counts**—an algebraic count from the polynomial and a geometric count from the eigenspace!

### Multiplicities of an Eigenvalue

Let $A$ be an $n \times n$ matrix with characteristic polynomial $p(\lambda) = \det(A - \lambda I)$. To determine whether $A$ is diagonalizable when roots repeat, we compare two numbers for each distinct eigenvalue $\lambda_i$:

> [!abstract] Definition: Algebraic and Geometric Multiplicity
> For each distinct eigenvalue $\lambda_i$ of an $n \times n$ matrix $A$:
> 1. **Algebraic Multiplicity ($AM(\lambda_i)$):** The number of times $\lambda_i$ is repeated as a root of the characteristic polynomial $p(\lambda) = \det(A - \lambda I) = 0$. Factoring $p(\lambda)$ over $\mathbb{C}$ into $m$ distinct roots $\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_m$:
>    $$
>    p(\lambda) = (-1)^n (\lambda - \lambda_1)^{d_1} (\lambda - \lambda_2)^{d_2} \cdots (\lambda - \lambda_m)^{d_m}, \quad AM(\lambda_i) = d_i
>    $$
>    By the Fundamental Theorem of Algebra, the algebraic multiplicities always sum to $n$:
>    $$
>    \sum_{i=1}^m AM(\lambda_i) = n
>    $$
> 2. **Geometric Multiplicity ($GM(\lambda_i)$):** The dimension of the eigenspace $E_{\lambda_i} = N(A - \lambda_i I)$, which is the maximum number of linearly independent eigenvectors associated with $\lambda_i$:
>    $$
>    GM(\lambda_i) = \dim N(A - \lambda_i I) = n - \operatorname{rank}(A - \lambda_i I)
>    $$

In simple terms, $AM(\lambda_i)$ is how many eigenvectors the characteristic polynomial *promises* for $\lambda_i$, while $GM(\lambda_i)$ is how many independent eigenvectors the nullspace $N(A - \lambda_i I)$ *actually delivers*. And as the next theorem shows, the nullspace can never deliver more independent eigenvectors than the polynomial promises.

> [!theorem] Fundamental Multiplicity Inequality
> For every eigenvalue $\lambda$ of any square matrix $A$:
> $$
> 1 \le GM(\lambda) \le AM(\lambda)
> $$

**Proof:**
First, since $\lambda$ is an eigenvalue of $A$, $\det(A - \lambda I) = 0$, which means the nullspace $N(A - \lambda I)$ contains at least one non-zero eigenvector; thus $GM(\lambda) = \dim N(A - \lambda I) \ge 1$.

To prove the upper bound $GM(\lambda) \le AM(\lambda)$, let $k = GM(\lambda) = \dim N(A - \lambda I) \ge 1$. We can choose an orthonormal basis $\{v_1, \dots, v_k\}$ for the eigenspace $N(A - \lambda I)$ and extend it (for instance, by Gram-Schmidt) to a full orthonormal basis $\{v_1, \dots, v_k, v_{k+1}, \dots, v_n\}$ for all of $\mathbb{C}^n$. Let's pack these basis vectors into an $n \times n$ unitary matrix $V = \begin{bmatrix} V_1 & V_2 \end{bmatrix}$, where the first $k$ columns form $V_1 = \begin{bmatrix} v_1 & \dots & v_k \end{bmatrix}$ and the remaining $n - k$ columns form $V_2 = \begin{bmatrix} v_{k+1} & \dots & v_n \end{bmatrix}$.

Now let's see what happens when we perform a unitary similarity transformation $V^{-1} A V = V^* A V$ in block form:
$$
V^{-1} A V = V^* A V = \begin{bmatrix} V_1^* A V_1 & V_1^* A V_2 \\ V_2^* A V_1 & V_2^* A V_2 \end{bmatrix}
$$
Because every column of $V_1$ is an eigenvector of $A$ with eigenvalue $\lambda$ ($A v_j = \lambda v_j$), the entire first block satisfies $A V_1 = \lambda V_1$. Using the orthonormality relations $V_1^* V_1 = I_k$ and $V_2^* V_1 = 0$, the left-hand blocks simplify immediately:
$$
V_1^* A V_1 = V_1^* (\lambda V_1) = \lambda V_1^* V_1 = \lambda I_k
$$
$$
V_2^* A V_1 = V_2^* (\lambda V_1) = \lambda V_2^* V_1 = 0
$$
Putting this together, the transformed matrix $V^{-1} A V$ is block upper-triangular:
$$
V^{-1} A V = \begin{bmatrix} \lambda I_k & B \\ 0 & C \end{bmatrix}
$$
where $B = V_1^* A V_2$ and $C = V_2^* A V_2$. Now let's compute the characteristic polynomial of $A$ in a variable $t$. Because $\det(V^{-1}) \det(V) = 1$, similarity transformations preserve determinants:
$$
\det(A - t I) = \det(V^{-1} A V - t I) = \det \begin{bmatrix} (\lambda - t)I_k & B \\ 0 & C - t I \end{bmatrix} = (\lambda - t)^k \det(C - t I)
$$
Look at that factor $(\lambda - t)^k$ sitting out in front: even before we inspect $\det(C - t I)$ (which might contain additional factors of $\lambda - t$), $(\lambda - t)^k$ already divides the characteristic polynomial $\det(A - t I)$! Therefore, the root $t = \lambda$ must have multiplicity at least $k$, which proves:
$$
AM(\lambda) \ge k = GM(\lambda) \quad \blacksquare
$$

### General Diagonalizability Criterion and Defective Matrices

Now let's put the pieces together. The total number of eigenvalues (counting algebraic multiplicity) is always $\sum_{i=1}^m AM(\lambda_i) = n$. Meanwhile, eigenvectors belonging to different eigenspaces $E_{\lambda_i}$ are linearly independent of one another, so the total number of linearly independent eigenvectors across the entire matrix is $\sum_{i=1}^m GM(\lambda_i)$. Since $GM(\lambda_i) \le AM(\lambda_i)$ for each eigenvalue, the only way the geometric multiplicities can add up to $n$ is if *not a single eigenvalue falls short*!

> [!theorem] General Diagonalizability Criterion
> An $n \times n$ matrix $A$ is diagonalizable if and only if for every distinct eigenvalue $\lambda_i$:
> $$
> GM(\lambda_i) = AM(\lambda_i)
> $$
> or equivalently, the dimensions of the eigenspaces sum to $n$ (so $\mathbb{C}^n = E_{\lambda_1} \oplus E_{\lambda_2} \oplus \dots \oplus E_{\lambda_m}$):
> $$
> \sum_{i=1}^m GM(\lambda_i) = n
> $$

> [!warning] Defective Matrices (Shortage of Eigenvectors)
> If $GM(\lambda_i) < AM(\lambda_i)$ for even one eigenvalue, the matrix $A$ suffers from a shortage of eigenvectors and is called **defective** (or non-diagonalizable). A defective matrix has fewer than $n$ linearly independent eigenvectors, making it impossible to form an invertible eigenvector matrix $X$.
> 
> **Canonical Example: The Nilpotent Shear Matrix**
> $$
> N = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}
> $$
> Its characteristic polynomial is $\det(N - \lambda I) = \lambda^2 = 0$, giving a single eigenvalue $\lambda = 0$ with algebraic multiplicity $AM(0) = 2$. However, when we look for the eigenspace $N(N - 0I)$:
> $$
> N - 0I = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} \implies \operatorname{rank}(N) = 1 \implies GM(0) = 2 - 1 = 1
> $$
> The entire eigenspace is just the 1-dimensional horizontal line spanned by $x = \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix}$. Because $GM(0) = 1 < AM(0) = 2$, $N$ lacks a second independent eigenvector and cannot be diagonalized.
> 
> **Families of Defective $2 \times 2$ Matrices with $\lambda = 5$:**
> A matrix does not have to be triangular or have zero eigenvalues to be defective. Consider the three matrices:
> $$
