When we work with an arbitrary square matrix $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$, its eigenvalue picture can get messy very quickly: even if every entry of $A$ is real, its eigenvalues can easily turn out to be complex numbers (think of a $90^\circ$ rotation matrix), its eigenvectors can meet at awkward, skewed angles, or—worst of all—the matrix might be defective and fail to have enough eigenvectors to span $\mathbb{R}^n$ at all.

The moment we require a matrix to be **real and symmetric** ($S = S^T \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$), every single one of those headaches disappears. Real symmetric matrices are the crown jewel of both pure and applied linear algebra because their structural symmetry across the main diagonal enforces two miraculous spectral properties:
1. **Every eigenvalue is guaranteed to be a real number** ($\lambda_i \in \mathbb{R}$).
2. **The eigenvectors can always be chosen to be mutually perpendicular unit vectors**, forming an orthonormal basis of $\mathbb{R}^n$.

In other words, while a general diagonalizable matrix factors as $A = X \Lambda X^{-1}$ with an arbitrary invertible eigenvector matrix $X$, a symmetric matrix replaces $X$ with an **orthogonal matrix** $Q$ ($Q^{-1} = Q^T$), giving the famous orthogonal diagonalization $S = Q \Lambda Q^T$. And within the family of symmetric matrices lives an even more special subclass: **symmetric positive definite matrices** ($S \succ 0$), where every eigenvalue is strictly positive ($\lambda_i > 0$). Positive definite matrices are the matrix counterpart to positive real numbers, governing everything from energy minimization and convex optimization to stability in dynamical systems and curved metric geometry.

## The Real Spectral Theorem

Let's begin by stating the centerpiece theorem for symmetric matrices—the **Spectral Theorem**—and then build up its complete proof step by step.

> [!theorem] The Spectral Theorem for Real Symmetric Matrices
> Every real symmetric matrix $S = S^T \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ can be factored into:
> $$
> S = Q \Lambda Q^{-1} = Q \Lambda Q^T
> $$
> where:
> 1. $\Lambda = \operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \dots, \lambda_n)$ is a real diagonal matrix containing the $n$ real eigenvalues of $S$.
> 2. $Q = \begin{bmatrix} q_1 & q_2 & \cdots & q_n \end{bmatrix}$ is a real orthogonal matrix whose columns $q_i$ are orthonormal eigenvectors of $S$ forming an orthonormal basis of $\mathbb{R}^n$ ($Q^T Q = Q Q^T = I$).
> 
> Conversely, any matrix of the form $Q \Lambda Q^T$ with an orthogonal matrix $Q$ and a real diagonal matrix $\Lambda$ is automatically symmetric:
> $$
> (Q \Lambda Q^T)^T = (Q^T)^T \Lambda^T Q^T = Q \Lambda Q^T
> $$

Why does $S = S^T$ guarantee real eigenvalues, perpendicular eigenvectors, and zero risk of missing eigenvectors when eigenvalues repeat? Let's unpack each of these three pillars in turn.

### Reality of Eigenvalues

Even though all entries of $S$ are real numbers, the characteristic polynomial $\det(S - \lambda I) = 0$ is a degree-$n$ polynomial whose roots $\lambda$ could *a priori* be complex numbers $\lambda \in \mathbb{C}$, accompanied by complex eigenvectors $x \in \mathbb{C}^n$. (After all, that is exactly what happens for a real rotation matrix!) So to prove that the eigenvalues of $S$ are strictly real, we must allow $\lambda$ and $x$ to be potentially complex at the start, test the eigenvalue equation $S x = \lambda x$ with the **complex conjugate transpose** $\bar{x}^T = x^H$, and watch how the symmetry ($S^T = S$) and reality ($\bar{S} = S$) of $S$ force the imaginary part of $\lambda$ to vanish.

> [!theorem] Reality of the Spectrum
> If $S \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ is symmetric ($S = S^T$), then all of its eigenvalues are real numbers ($\lambda \in \mathbb{R}$), and its eigenvectors can be chosen to have purely real components ($x \in \mathbb{R}^n$).

**Proof:**
Suppose $\lambda \in \mathbb{C}$ is an eigenvalue of $S$ with a potentially complex non-zero eigenvector $x \in \mathbb{C}^n$:
$$
S x = \lambda x \quad (x \ne 0)
$$
To turn this vector equation into a scalar equation whose complex conjugate we can inspect, premultiply both sides by the conjugate transpose (Hermitian transpose) $\bar{x}^T = x^H$:
$$
\bar{x}^T S x = \lambda \bar{x}^T x
$$
Now let's take the complex conjugate of both sides. Because every entry of $S$ is a real number, $S$ is unaffected by complex conjugation ($\bar{S} = S$), which gives:
$$
\overline{\bar{x}^T S x} = x^T \bar{S} \bar{x} = x^T S \bar{x} = \bar{\lambda} x^T \bar{x}
$$
Look closely at the middle scalar quantity $x^T S \bar{x}$. Because it is a $1 \times 1$ scalar, taking its transpose leaves it completely unchanged! Using the symmetry of $S$ ($S^T = S$), we find:
$$
x^T S \bar{x} = (x^T S \bar{x})^T = \bar{x}^T S^T x = \bar{x}^T S x
$$
Notice what just happened: $\bar{x}^T S x$ is equal to its own complex conjugate $x^T S \bar{x}$. Since $x^T \bar{x} = \bar{x}^T x$, equating our two expressions for $\bar{x}^T S x$ yields:
$$
\lambda \bar{x}^T x = \bar{\lambda} \bar{x}^T x \implies (\lambda - \bar{\lambda}) \bar{x}^T x = 0
$$
Could the inner product $\bar{x}^T x$ be zero? No! Because $x \ne 0$ and we used the *conjugate* transpose $\bar{x}^T$, the inner product is a sum of squared magnitudes:
$$
\bar{x}^T x = \sum_{j=1}^n \bar{x}_j x_j = \sum_{j=1}^n |x_j|^2 > 0
$$
Dividing by the strictly positive real number $\bar{x}^T x$ forces:
$$
\lambda - \bar{\lambda} = 0 \implies \lambda = \bar{\lambda}
$$
Since $\lambda$ equals its own complex conjugate, $\lambda \in \mathbb{R}$ is strictly real. Finally, once we know $\lambda$ is real, the homogeneous linear system $(S - \lambda I) x = 0$ has purely real coefficients; Gaussian elimination on a singular real matrix $S - \lambda I$ stays entirely within $\mathbb{R}$ and always produces at least one real non-zero eigenvector $x \in \mathbb{R}^n$ (and in the trivial case $S = O$, every non-zero vector $x \in \mathbb{R}^n$ is already a real eigenvector with $\lambda = 0$). $\blacksquare$

### Orthogonality of Eigenvectors

Now that we know the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of $S$ live entirely in $\mathbb{R}$ and $\mathbb{R}^n$, what happens when we compare two eigenvectors belonging to *different* eigenvalues? For a general matrix, those eigenvectors can point at any angle relative to one another. For a symmetric matrix, however, they are forced to meet at a $90^\circ$ right angle!

> [!theorem] Orthogonality of Distinct Eigenspaces
> Eigenvectors of a real symmetric matrix $S = S^T$ corresponding to distinct eigenvalues ($\lambda_1 \ne \lambda_2$) are mutually orthogonal ($x^T y = 0$).

**Proof:**
Suppose $S x = \lambda_1 x$ and $S y = \lambda_2 y$, where $\lambda_1 \ne \lambda_2$ and $x, y \ne 0$. The key is to evaluate the scalar bilinear product $x^T S y$ in two different ways—once by letting $S$ act on $y$ to the right, and once by letting $S$ act on $x$ to the left.

First, grouping $S$ with $y$ and using $S y = \lambda_2 y$ gives:
$$
x^T (S y) = x^T (\lambda_2 y) = \lambda_2 (x^T y)
$$
Second, using the symmetry $S = S^T$ to move $S$ onto $x$ via $(S x)^T = x^T S^T = x^T S$ gives:
$$
(S x)^T y = (\lambda_1 x)^T y = \lambda_1 (x^T y)
$$
Because both expressions compute the exact same scalar $x^T S y = (S x)^T y$, they must be equal:
$$
\lambda_1 (x^T y) = \lambda_2 (x^T y) \implies (\lambda_1 - \lambda_2)(x^T y) = 0
$$
Since the eigenvalues are distinct ($\lambda_1 - \lambda_2 \ne 0$), we can divide by $\lambda_1 - \lambda_2$ to conclude:
$$
x^T y = 0
$$
Thus, eigenvectors corresponding to distinct eigenvalues are strictly orthogonal. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Fundamental Subspaces Proof of Orthogonality
> That three-line algebraic proof is wonderfully slick, but *why* does symmetry force eigenvectors from different eigenspaces to be perpendicular geometrically? We can see the exact reason by looking at the four fundamental subspaces of a shifted matrix!
> 
> Let $S x = \lambda_1 x$ and $S y = \lambda_2 y$ with $\lambda_1 \ne \lambda_2$, and consider the shifted symmetric matrix:
> $$
> A = S - \lambda_2 I
> $$
> Where do the two eigenvectors $y$ and $x$ live relative to $A$?
> - The eigenvector $y$ satisfies $A y = (S - \lambda_2 I)y = 0$, so $y$ lies directly in the **nullspace** $N(A)$.
> - The eigenvector $x$ satisfies $A x = (S - \lambda_2 I)x = (\lambda_1 - \lambda_2)x$. Because $\lambda_1 - \lambda_2 \ne 0$, we can rewrite this as $x = \frac{1}{\lambda_1 - \lambda_2} A x$, which shows that $x$ is a linear combination of the columns of $A$—so $x$ lies in the **column space** $C(A)$!
> 
> Now bring in the Fundamental Theorem of Linear Algebra: for *any* matrix $A$, the row space $C(A^T)$ is orthogonal to the nullspace $N(A)$. Because $A = S - \lambda_2 I$ is symmetric ($A^T = A$), its column space is identical to its row space:
> $$
> C(A) = C(A^T) \perp N(A)
> $$
> Since $x \in C(A)$ and $y \in N(A)$, it immediately follows that $x \perp y$, meaning $x^T y = 0$!

### Diagonalizability and Schur's Triangularization

At this point, if a symmetric matrix $S \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ happens to have $n$ *distinct* real eigenvalues $\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_n$, we are already done: its $n$ unit eigenvectors $q_1, \dots, q_n$ are mutually orthogonal, so assembling them as columns gives an orthogonal matrix $Q$ with $S = Q \Lambda Q^T$.

*But what if an eigenvalue repeats?* For a general matrix, a repeated eigenvalue can cause a shortage of eigenvectors (geometric multiplicity $<$ algebraic multiplicity), making the matrix defective and non-diagonalizable. How do we prove that a symmetric matrix *never* suffers from a shortage of eigenvectors, no matter how many times an eigenvalue repeats?

The cleanest, most powerful way to settle this once and for all is **Schur's Triangularization Theorem**. Schur's theorem shows that *every* square matrix $A$—even a defective one!—can be brought to upper triangular form $T$ via a unitary (or orthogonal) similarity transformation $A = U T U^H$. Once we have Schur's triangular form in hand, applying it to a symmetric matrix $S = S^T$ will force the upper triangular matrix $T$ to be symmetric as well—instantly collapsing $T$ into the diagonal matrix $\Lambda$!

> [!theorem] Schur's Triangularization Theorem
> For every square matrix $A \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n}$ with eigenvalues $\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_n$, there exists a unitary matrix $U$ ($U^H U = I$) such that:
> $$
> A = U T U^H \quad \left(\text{or } U^H A U = T\right)
> $$
> where $T$ is upper triangular with the eigenvalues $\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_n$ on its main diagonal. Moreover, if $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ is a real matrix whose eigenvalues are all real, the transformation matrix can be chosen to be a real orthogonal matrix $Q$ ($Q^T Q = I$):
> $$
> A = Q T Q^T
> $$

**Proof:**
We proceed by mathematical induction on the matrix dimension $n$, peeling off one eigenvalue and one orthonormal basis direction at a time.

1. **Base Case ($n = 1$):**
   For a $1 \times 1$ matrix $A = [a]$, the matrix is already trivially upper triangular. Choosing the $1 \times 1$ unitary matrix $U = [1]$ gives:
   $$
   U^H A U = [1]^H [a] [1] = [a] = T
   $$
   whose single diagonal entry is the eigenvalue $\lambda_1 = a$.

2. **Induction Hypothesis:**
   Assume that the theorem holds for all $(n - 1) \times (n - 1)$ matrices. That is, every square matrix of size $n - 1$ is unitarily similar to an upper triangular matrix whose diagonal entries are its eigenvalues.

3. **Inductive Step:**
   Let $A$ be an $n \times n$ matrix. By the Fundamental Theorem of Algebra, the characteristic equation $\det(A - \lambda I) = 0$ has at least one root $\lambda_1 \in \mathbb{C}$, and therefore $A$ has at least one unit eigenvector $q_1 \in \mathbb{C}^n$:
   $$
   A q_1 = \lambda_1 q_1 \quad \text{with } \|q_1\| = 1
   $$
   Even if $A$ has no other independent eigenvectors, we can always use the Gram-Schmidt process to extend the single unit vector $\{q_1\}$ to a full orthonormal basis $\{q_1, v_2, \dots, v_n\}$ of $\mathbb{C}^n$. Let's pack these basis vectors into an $n \times n$ unitary matrix $U_1$:
   $$
   U_1 = \begin{bmatrix} q_1 & V \end{bmatrix} \quad \text{where } V = \begin{bmatrix} v_2 & \dots & v_n \end{bmatrix} \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times (n-1)}
   $$
   Because the columns of $U_1$ are orthonormal ($U_1^H U_1 = I_n$), we have $q_1^H q_1 = 1$, $V^H q_1 = \mathbf{0}$ (since $v_2, \dots, v_n$ are all orthogonal to $q_1$), and $V^H V = I_{n-1}$.
   
   Now let's see what happens when we compute the similarity transformation $U_1^H A U_1$ using $A q_1 = \lambda_1 q_1$:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   U_1^H A U_1 &= \begin{bmatrix} q_1^H \\ V^H \end{bmatrix} A \begin{bmatrix} q_1 & V \end{bmatrix} \\
   &= \begin{bmatrix} q_1^H \\ V^H \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} A q_1 & A V \end{bmatrix} \\
   &= \begin{bmatrix} q_1^H \\ V^H \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \lambda_1 q_1 & A V \end{bmatrix} \\
   &= \begin{bmatrix} \lambda_1 q_1^H q_1 & q_1^H A V \\ \lambda_1 V^H q_1 & V^H A V \end{bmatrix}
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   Substituting $q_1^H q_1 = 1$ and $V^H q_1 = \mathbf{0}$ clears out the entire first column below $\lambda_1$:
   $$
   U_1^H A U_1 = \begin{bmatrix} \lambda_1 & w^H \\ \mathbf{0} & A_1 \end{bmatrix}
   $$
   where $w^H = q_1^H A V \in \mathbb{C}^{1 \times (n-1)}$ and $A_1 = V^H A V \in \mathbb{C}^{(n-1) \times (n-1)}$.
   
   Look at that lower-right block $A_1$: it is an $(n - 1) \times (n - 1)$ matrix whose eigenvalues are the remaining eigenvalues $\lambda_2, \dots, \lambda_n$ of $A$. By our induction hypothesis, there exists an $(n - 1) \times (n - 1)$ unitary matrix $\tilde{U}$ that triangularizes $A_1$:
   $$
   \tilde{U}^H A_1 \tilde{U} = T_1
   $$
   where $T_1$ is an $(n - 1) \times (n - 1)$ upper triangular matrix with $\lambda_2, \dots, \lambda_n$ on its main diagonal.
   
   To apply $\tilde{U}$ to the lower-right $(n-1) \times (n-1)$ block without disturbing the first column we already cleaned up, we embed $\tilde{U}$ into an $n \times n$ block matrix:
   $$
   U_2 = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \mathbf{0}^T \\ \mathbf{0} & \tilde{U} \end{bmatrix}
   $$
   Notice that $U_2$ is itself unitary:
   $$
   U_2^H U_2 = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \mathbf{0}^T \\ \mathbf{0} & \tilde{U}^H \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \mathbf{0}^T \\ \mathbf{0} & \tilde{U} \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \mathbf{0}^T \\ \mathbf{0} & \tilde{U}^H \tilde{U} \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \mathbf{0}^T \\ \mathbf{0} & I_{n-1} \end{bmatrix} = I_n
   $$
   Therefore, the combined transformation matrix:
   $$
   U = U_1 U_2
   $$
   is the product of two unitary matrices and is consequently unitary ($U^H U = U_2^H U_1^H U_1 U_2 = U_2^H I U_2 = I$). Applying the similarity transformation with $U = U_1 U_2$ gives:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   U^H A U &= (U_1 U_2)^H A (U_1 U_2) \\
   &= U_2^H (U_1^H A U_1) U_2 \\
   &= \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \mathbf{0}^T \\ \mathbf{0} & \tilde{U}^H \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \lambda_1 & w^H \\ \mathbf{0} & A_1 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \mathbf{0}^T \\ \mathbf{0} & \tilde{U} \end{bmatrix} \\
   &= \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \mathbf{0}^T \\ \mathbf{0} & \tilde{U}^H \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \lambda_1 & w^H \tilde{U} \\ \mathbf{0} & A_1 \tilde{U} \end{bmatrix} \\
   &= \begin{bmatrix} \lambda_1 & w^H \tilde{U} \\ \mathbf{0} & \tilde{U}^H A_1 \tilde{U} \end{bmatrix} \\
   &= \begin{bmatrix} \lambda_1 & w^H \tilde{U} \\ \mathbf{0} & T_1 \end{bmatrix} = T
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   Since $T_1$ is upper triangular, the entire $n \times n$ matrix $T$ is upper triangular with diagonal entries $\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \dots, \lambda_n$. Multiplying on the left by $U$ and on the right by $U^H$ yields $A = U T U^H$, completing the induction.

4. **Specialization to a Real Matrix with Real Eigenvalues ($A = Q T Q^T$):**
   What if $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ is a real matrix and all of its eigenvalues $\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_n$ are real? Then every step of our inductive construction stays inside the real numbers! Specifically, because $\lambda_1 \in \mathbb{R}$, the shifted matrix $A - \lambda_1 I$ is purely real, so its nullspace contains a real unit eigenvector $q_1 \in \mathbb{R}^n$. Extending $\{q_1\}$ via Gram-Schmidt in $\mathbb{R}^n$ produces a real orthonormal extension $V \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times (n-1)}$, making $U_1 = \begin{bmatrix} q_1 & V \end{bmatrix}$ a real orthogonal matrix. Consequently, $A_1 = V^T A V \in \mathbb{R}^{(n-1) \times (n-1)}$ is a real matrix whose eigenvalues $\lambda_2, \dots, \lambda_n$ are also real. By the induction hypothesis, $\tilde{U}$ is real orthogonal, which makes $U_2$ and the product $Q = U_1 U_2$ real orthogonal matrices, yielding $A = Q T Q^T$. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Application to Symmetric Matrices: Completing the Spectral Theorem
> Look at how effortlessly Schur's theorem finishes the proof of the **Real Spectral Theorem**!
> 
> Suppose $S \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ is symmetric ($S = S^T$). We already proved that all of its eigenvalues are real, so Schur's theorem guarantees an orthogonal matrix $Q$ and a real upper triangular matrix $T$ such that $S = Q T Q^T$, or equivalently $T = Q^T S Q$. Now let's take the transpose of $T$:
> $$
> T^T = (Q^T S Q)^T = Q^T S^T (Q^T)^T = Q^T S Q = T
> $$
> Think about what $T^T = T$ means: $T$ is simultaneously **upper triangular** (all entries below the main diagonal are zero) and **symmetric** (entries above the diagonal mirror the entries below the diagonal). The only way an upper triangular matrix can be symmetric is if every strictly off-diagonal entry is zero:
> $$
> t_{ij} = 0 \quad \text{for all } i \ne j
> $$
> Thus, $T = \Lambda$ is strictly diagonal, and $S = Q \Lambda Q^T$! This proves that *every* real symmetric matrix possesses a complete set of $n$ orthonormal eigenvectors, with zero possibility of a defective eigenvalue ($GM = AM$ always), even when eigenvalues are repeated.

### Spectral Decomposition into Projection Matrices

Let's look at the factorization $S = Q \Lambda Q^T$ from a column-by-row perspective. Back in Section 6.2, we saw that any diagonalizable matrix $A = X \Lambda X^{-1} = X \Lambda Y^T$ breaks into a sum of rank-one outer products $\sum_{i=1}^n \lambda_i x_i y_i^T$ built from right eigenvectors $x_i$ and left eigenvectors $y_i$. When the matrix is not symmetric, $x_i$ and $y_i$ are different vectors, and each outer product $x_i y_i^T$ is an *oblique* projection along skewed eigenspaces.

For a symmetric matrix $S = Q \Lambda Q^T$, however, $Q^{-1} = Q^T$, so the left and right eigenvectors are the exact same orthonormal vectors $q_i$! Expanding the matrix product column-by-row turns $S$ into a weighted sum of **orthogonal projection matrices**:
$$
\begin{aligned}
S &= \begin{bmatrix} q_1 & q_2 & \cdots & q_n \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \lambda_1 & & & 0 \\ & \lambda_2 & & \\ & & \ddots & \\ 0 & & & \lambda_n \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} q_1^T \\ q_2^T \\ \vdots \\ q_n^T \end{bmatrix} \\
  &= \lambda_1 q_1 q_1^T + \lambda_2 q_2 q_2^T + \cdots + \lambda_n q_n q_n^T \\
  &= \sum_{i=1}^n \lambda_i P_i
\end{aligned}
$$
where each rank-one matrix $P_i = q_i q_i^T$ is the orthogonal projection matrix onto the one-dimensional eigenspace spanned by the unit eigenvector $q_i$. Geometrically, multiplying any vector $x$ by $S$ simply decomposes $x$ into its orthogonal projections $P_i x = (q_i^T x) q_i$ along the $n$ perpendicular eigenvector axes, scales each component by its eigenvalue $\lambda_i$, and adds them back together.

> [!abstract] Properties of Spectral Projectors
> Because the eigenvectors $q_1, \dots, q_n$ are orthonormal ($q_i^T q_j = \delta_{ij}$), the orthogonal projection matrices $P_i = q_i q_i^T$ satisfy four clean algebraic identities:
> 1. **Idempotence & Symmetry (Self-Adjointness):** Projecting twice onto $q_i$ is the same as projecting once, and the projector is symmetric:
>    $$
>    P_i^2 = (q_i q_i^T)(q_i q_i^T) = q_i (q_i^T q_i) q_i^T = q_i (1) q_i^T = P_i, \qquad P_i^T = (q_i q_i^T)^T = q_i q_i^T = P_i
>    $$
> 2. **Mutual Orthogonality (Annihilation):** Projecting onto $q_j$ and then onto a perpendicular axis $q_i$ ($i \ne j$) gives zero:
>    $$
>    P_i P_j = q_i (q_i^T q_j) q_j^T = q_i (0) q_j^T = O \quad \text{for all } i \ne j
>    $$
> 3. **Resolution of the Identity:** Summing all $n$ perpendicular projections reconstructs the entire space $\mathbb{R}^n$:
>    $$
>    \sum_{i=1}^n P_i = \sum_{i=1}^n q_i q_i^T = Q Q^T = I
>    $$
> 4. **Rank-One Structure:** Each $P_i$ projects onto a line, so $\operatorname{rank}(P_i) = 1$ and $\operatorname{tr}(P_i) = \operatorname{tr}(q_i q_i^T) = q_i^T q_i = 1$.

## The Five Equivalent Tests for Positive Definiteness

Now that we know every real symmetric matrix $S = S^T$ has real eigenvalues $\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_n$, a natural question arises: *When are all $n$ eigenvalues strictly positive ($\lambda_i > 0$)?*

A symmetric matrix whose eigenvalues are all strictly positive is called **symmetric positive definite** (denoted $S \succ 0$). Finding the eigenvalues of a large matrix directly requires solving the degree-$n$ characteristic polynomial $\det(S - \lambda I) = 0$, which is computationally expensive and algebraically intractable for $n \ge 5$. Remarkably, however, we don't have to compute eigenvalues at all to test whether $S \succ 0$! Positive definiteness unifies the entire first half of linear algebra—linking **eigenvalues**, **quadratic energy**, **least-squares factorizations ($A^T A$)**, **determinants**, and **elimination pivots** into five mutually equivalent criteria.

> [!theorem] Master Theorem: Five Equivalent Tests for $S \succ 0$
> For any real symmetric matrix $S = S^T \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$, the following five conditions are mathematically equivalent (if $S$ satisfies any one of them, it satisfies all five and is **positive definite**, $S \succ 0$):
> 1. **Eigenvalue Test:** All $n$ eigenvalues of $S$ are strictly positive:
>    $$
>    \lambda_i > 0 \quad \text{for all } i = 1, 2, \dots, n
>    $$
> 2. **Energy / Quadratic Form Test:** The quadratic form (or "energy") is strictly positive for every non-zero vector $x \in \mathbb{R}^n$:
>    $$
>    x^T S x > 0 \quad \text{for all } x \ne 0
>    $$
> 3. **Full-Rank Gram Factorization Test:** $S$ can be factored as:
>    $$
>    S = A^T A
>    $$
>    for some matrix $A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ having linearly independent columns (i.e., full column rank $\operatorname{rank}(A) = n$, so $N(A) = \{0\}$).
> 4. **Leading Determinants Test (Sylvester's Criterion):** All $n$ leading principal minors (upper-left determinants) are strictly positive:
>    $$
>    D_k = \det(S_k) > 0 \quad \text{for } k = 1, 2, \dots, n
>    $$
>    where $S_k$ is the $k \times k$ submatrix in the top-left corner of $S$.
> 5. **Pivot Test:** Gaussian elimination without row exchanges completes with all $n$ pivots strictly positive:
>    $$
>    d_k > 0 \quad \text{for } k = 1, 2, \dots, n
>    $$

### Analytical Proof of Equivalence

**Proof:**
To understand *why* these five seemingly different concepts—eigenvalues, quadratic forms, $A^T A$, determinants, and pivots—all say the exact same thing, let's prove their equivalence step by step.

#### 1. Equivalence of Test 1 (Eigenvalues) and Test 2 (Energy)
Why is having positive eigenvalues ($\lambda_i > 0$) identical to having positive quadratic energy ($x^T S x > 0$ for all $x \ne 0$)?
- **$(1 \implies 2)$:** Suppose all eigenvalues satisfy $\lambda_i > 0$. By the Spectral Theorem, the eigenvectors $q_1, \dots, q_n$ of $S$ form an orthonormal basis of $\mathbb{R}^n$. That means any non-zero vector $x \in \mathbb{R}^n$ can be uniquely expanded in the eigenvector basis:
  $$
  x = c_1 q_1 + c_2 q_2 + \cdots + c_n q_n
  $$
  where $\|x\|^2 = \sum_{i=1}^n c_i^2 > 0$ (so at least one coefficient $c_i$ is non-zero). Now let's evaluate the quadratic energy $x^T S x$ in this eigenbasis:
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  x^T S x &= \left( \sum_{i=1}^n c_i q_i \right)^T S \left( \sum_{j=1}^n c_j q_j \right) \\
          &= \left( \sum_{i=1}^n c_i q_i^T \right) \left( \sum_{j=1}^n c_j \lambda_j q_j \right) \\
          &= \sum_{i=1}^n \sum_{j=1}^n c_i c_j \lambda_j (q_i^T q_j)
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  Because the eigenvectors are orthonormal ($q_i^T q_j = \delta_{ij}$), every cross-term with $i \ne j$ drops out completely, leaving a pure sum of squares weighted by the eigenvalues:
  $$
  x^T S x = \sum_{i=1}^n \lambda_i c_i^2 = \lambda_1 c_1^2 + \lambda_2 c_2^2 + \cdots + \lambda_n c_n^2
  $$
  Since every $\lambda_i > 0$, every $c_i^2 \ge 0$, and at least one $c_i^2 > 0$, this sum is guaranteed to be strictly positive: $x^T S x > 0$.
- **$(2 \implies 1)$:** Conversely, assume $x^T S x > 0$ for every non-zero vector $x \ne 0$. To check the sign of the $i$-th eigenvalue $\lambda_i$, we simply plug its corresponding eigenvector $x = q_i \ne 0$ into the energy form:
  $$
  q_i^T S q_i = q_i^T (\lambda_i q_i) = \lambda_i (q_i^T q_i) = \lambda_i \|q_i\|^2
  $$
  Because $q_i^T S q_i > 0$ by hypothesis and $\|q_i\|^2 > 0$, dividing by $\|q_i\|^2$ immediately gives:
  $$
  \lambda_i = \frac{q_i^T S q_i}{\|q_i\|^2} > 0
  $$

#### 2. Equivalence of Test 2 (Energy) and Test 3 ($S = A^T A$)
Next, let's see why positive energy ($x^T S x > 0$) is equivalent to factoring $S$ as a Gram matrix $S = A^T A$ where $A$ has independent columns.
- **$(3 \implies 2)$:** Suppose $S = A^T A$, where the columns of $A$ are linearly independent ($N(A) = \{0\}$). When we plug $S = A^T A$ into the quadratic energy $x^T S x$, we can group one $A$ with $x^T$ and the other $A$ with $x$:
  $$
  x^T S x = x^T (A^T A) x = (A x)^T (A x) = \|A x\|^2
  $$
  Look at the right-hand side: $\|A x\|^2$ is the squared length of the vector $A x$, which is always $\ge 0$, and equals zero if and only if $A x = 0$. Because $A$ has linearly independent columns, its nullspace is trivial ($N(A) = \{0\}$), so $x \ne 0$ guarantees $A x \ne 0$. Therefore:
  $$
  x^T S x = \|A x\|^2 > 0 \quad \text{for all } x \ne 0
  $$
- **$(2 \implies 3)$:** Conversely, suppose $x^T S x > 0$ for all $x \ne 0$. By Test 1, all eigenvalues $\lambda_i$ of $S = Q \Lambda Q^T$ are strictly positive, which allows us to take their real square roots and form the positive diagonal matrix:
  $$
  \Lambda^{1/2} = \operatorname{diag}\left(\sqrt{\lambda_1}, \dots, \sqrt{\lambda_n}\right)
  $$
  Splitting $\Lambda = \Lambda^{1/2} \Lambda^{1/2}$ inside $S = Q \Lambda Q^T$ suggests defining $A = \Lambda^{1/2} Q^T$. Then:
  $$
  A^T A = (\Lambda^{1/2} Q^T)^T (\Lambda^{1/2} Q^T) = (Q \Lambda^{1/2})(\Lambda^{1/2} Q^T) = Q \Lambda Q^T = S
  $$
  Does $A$ have independent columns? Since $\det(A) = \det(\Lambda^{1/2}) \det(Q^T) = \left(\prod_{i=1}^n \sqrt{\lambda_i}\right)(\pm 1) \ne 0$, the $n \times n$ matrix $A$ is invertible and therefore has linearly independent columns! (Notice that we could also insert an extra $Q$ to define the symmetric matrix square root $A = S^{1/2} = Q \Lambda^{1/2} Q^T$, which satisfies $A^T A = S$ as well.)

> [!note] Non-Uniqueness of the $S = A^T A$ Factorization
> Notice that the factorization $S = A^T A$ in Test 3 is **not** unique—in fact, there are infinitely many matrices $A$ that produce the exact same positive definite matrix $S$! We already saw two choices ($A = \Lambda^{1/2} Q^T$ and the symmetric square root $A = Q \Lambda^{1/2} Q^T$), and shortly we will meet a third, upper triangular choice $A = R$ from Cholesky factorization.
> 
> More generally, if $A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ satisfies $A^T A = S$, then multiplying $A$ on the left by *any* orthogonal (or orthonormal-column) matrix $U$ with $U^T U = I$ leaves the product unchanged:
> $$
> A' = U A \implies (A')^T (A') = (U A)^T (U A) = A^T U^T U A = A^T I A = A^T A = S
> $$
> Geometrically, $A^T A$ records all the dot products (lengths and angles) between the columns of $A$, and rotating or reflecting those columns rigidly via $U$ preserves every single dot product.

#### 3. Equivalence of Test 4 (Leading Determinants) and Test 5 (Pivots)
How are the upper-left determinants $D_k = \det(S_k)$ connected to the pivots $d_k$ of Gaussian elimination?
When we perform Gaussian elimination without row exchanges on a symmetric matrix $S$, symmetry is preserved in the symmetric $L D L^T$ factorization:
$$
S = L D L^T
$$
where $L$ is unit lower triangular ($1$s on its main diagonal) and $D = \operatorname{diag}(d_1, d_2, \dots, d_n)$ is the diagonal matrix of pivots.

Now here is the key observation about Gaussian elimination: when we eliminate within the top-left $k \times k$ submatrix $S_k$, operations on the first $k$ rows and columns never touch rows or columns $k+1, \dots, n$. Therefore, the $L D L^T$ factorization of the $k \times k$ leading submatrix $S_k$ is formed simply by taking the top-left $k \times k$ blocks of $L$ and $D$:
$$
S_k = L_k D_k L_k^T
$$
where $L_k$ is unit lower triangular ($\det(L_k) = \det(L_k^T) = 1$) and $D_k = \operatorname{diag}(d_1, \dots, d_k)$. Taking the determinant of both sides gives:
$$
D_k = \det(S_k) = \det(L_k) \det(D_k) \det(L_k^T) = 1 \cdot (d_1 d_2 \cdots d_k) \cdot 1 = \prod_{j=1}^k d_j
$$
That is a fundamental identity in linear algebra: the $k$-th leading principal minor $D_k = \det(S_k)$ is simply the product of the first $k$ pivots!
$$
D_1 = d_1, \quad D_2 = d_1 d_2, \quad D_3 = d_1 d_2 d_3, \quad \dots, \quad D_n = d_1 d_2 \cdots d_n
$$
Dividing consecutive determinants immediately isolates each individual pivot as a ratio of determinants:
$$
d_1 = D_1, \quad d_k = \frac{D_k}{D_{k-1}} \quad \text{for } k = 2, 3, \dots, n \quad (\text{defining } D_0 = 1)
$$
From these two equations, the equivalence of Test 4 and Test 5 is immediate:
- **$(5 \implies 4)$:** If every pivot is strictly positive ($d_k > 0$), then every product $D_k = d_1 d_2 \cdots d_k > 0$ is strictly positive.
- **$(4 \implies 5)$:** Conversely, if every leading determinant is strictly positive ($D_k > 0$), then no zero pivot is ever encountered (so elimination completes without row exchanges) and every quotient $d_k = D_k / D_{k-1} > 0$ is strictly positive.

#### 4. Equivalence of Test 5 (Pivots) and Test 3 ($S = A^T A$)
Finally, let's link the elimination pivots $d_k > 0$ back to the factorization $S = A^T A$ (and Energy Test 2), closing our logical loop across all five tests!
- **$(5 \implies 3)$:** Suppose all $n$ pivots are strictly positive ($d_k > 0$). Then we can split the diagonal pivot matrix $D$ into two identical real square roots:
  $$
  D = D^{1/2} D^{1/2} = \operatorname{diag}\left(\sqrt{d_1}, \dots, \sqrt{d_n}\right) \operatorname{diag}\left(\sqrt{d_1}, \dots, \sqrt{d_n}\right)
  $$
  Substituting $D = D^{1/2} D^{1/2}$ into the symmetric elimination factorization $S = L D L^T$ gives:
  $$
  S = L (D^{1/2} D^{1/2}) L^T = (L D^{1/2})(D^{1/2} L^T) = (D^{1/2} L^T)^T (D^{1/2} L^T)
  $$
  If we define the upper triangular matrix:
  $$
  R = D^{1/2} L^T
  $$
  then $S = R^T R$! Because $L^T$ is unit upper triangular, the diagonal entries of $R$ are precisely $R_{kk} = \sqrt{d_k} > 0$. Thus $\det(R) = \prod_{k=1}^n \sqrt{d_k} > 0$, meaning $R$ is invertible with linearly independent columns. Setting $A = R$ satisfies Test 3!
- **$(3 \implies 5)$:** Conversely, suppose $S = A^T A$ with independent columns, which we already know implies the Energy Test $x^T S x > 0$ for all $x \ne 0$. Note that every leading submatrix $S_k$ also satisfies $x_k^T S_k x_k > 0$ (by restricting $x$ to its first $k$ components), so $\det(S_k) \ne 0$ and elimination $S = L D L^T$ proceeds to completion without row exchanges. Substituting $S = L D L^T$ into the quadratic energy $x^T S x$ gives:
  $$
  x^T S x = x^T (L D L^T) x = (L^T x)^T D (L^T x)
  $$
  Let's call the transformed vector $y = L^T x = \begin{bmatrix} y_1 & y_2 & \cdots & y_n \end{bmatrix}^T$. Because $D = \operatorname{diag}(d_1, d_2, \dots, d_n)$ is diagonal, the quadratic form expands into a sum of squares weighted by the pivots:
  $$
  x^T S x = y^T D y = d_1 y_1^2 + d_2 y_2^2 + \cdots + d_n y_n^2
  $$
  Notice how clean this is: Gaussian elimination $S = L D L^T$ is literally **completing the square** on the quadratic form $x^T S x$, and the coefficients outside the squares are the pivots $d_1, \dots, d_n$! Since $L^T$ is invertible, for any index $k$ we can choose $x = (L^T)^{-1} e_k \ne 0$ so that $y = L^T x = e_k$ (meaning $y_k = 1$ and all other $y_j = 0$). Plugging this vector into the energy inequality yields:
  $$
  0 < x^T S x = d_k (1)^2 = d_k
  $$
  Thus, every single pivot $d_k$ must be strictly positive! This completes the proof of all five equivalences. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Principal Minors vs. Leading Principal Minors
> In Test 4 (Sylvester's Criterion), we only had to check the $n$ **leading principal minors**—the determinants of the $k \times k$ submatrices formed by taking the *first* $k$ rows and the *first* $k$ columns of $S$ for $k = 1, 2, \dots, n$.
> 
> More generally, if we choose *any* subset of $k$ row indices $\{i_1, i_2, \dots, i_k\}$ and the *exact same* subset of $k$ column indices $\{i_1, i_2, \dots, i_k\}$, the resulting $k \times k$ symmetric submatrix $S_{\mathcal{I}}$ is called a **principal submatrix**, and its determinant $\det(S_{\mathcal{I}})$ is called a **principal minor**. Since each of the $n$ indices can be either included or excluded (excluding the empty set), an $n \times n$ matrix has $2^n - 1$ principal minors in total.
> 
> Using the Energy Test, it is easy to see that if $S \succ 0$ is positive definite, then **every principal submatrix $S_{\mathcal{I}}$ is also positive definite** (and therefore all $2^n - 1$ principal minors are strictly positive):
> - Choose any non-zero vector $x_k \in \mathbb{R}^k$, and embed it into a full vector $x \in \mathbb{R}^n \setminus \{0\}$ by placing the entries of $x_k$ at the index positions $\{i_1, i_2, \dots, i_k\}$ and setting all other $n - k$ components of $x$ to zero.
> - Because the zero components of $x$ wipe out every row and column of $S$ outside $\{i_1, i_2, \dots, i_k\}$, the quadratic form collapses directly to the submatrix:
>   $$
>   x_k^T S_{\mathcal{I}} x_k = x^T S x > 0 \quad \text{for all } x_k \ne 0
>   $$
> - Therefore, $S_{\mathcal{I}} \succ 0$ and $\det(S_{\mathcal{I}}) > 0$! The miracle of Sylvester's Criterion is that to *guarantee* all $2^n - 1$ principal minors are positive, we only ever need to test the $n$ leading ones in the upper-left corner.

### The $2 \times 2$ Case

To see all five tests working together in complete detail, let's examine the general $2 \times 2$ real symmetric matrix:
$$
S = \begin{bmatrix} a & b \\ b & c \end{bmatrix}
$$
When is $S$ positive definite? Let's run it through our criteria:
1. **Leading Determinants:** The $1 \times 1$ upper-left determinant is $D_1 = a > 0$, and the $2 \times 2$ determinant is $D_2 = \det(S) = a c - b^2 > 0$.
2. **Pivots:** The first pivot is $d_1 = a > 0$. Subtracting $\frac{b}{a}$ times row 1 from row 2 eliminates the $(2, 1)$ entry and leaves the second pivot in the $(2, 2)$ corner:
   $$
   d_2 = c - \frac{b^2}{a} = \frac{a c - b^2}{a} > 0
   $$
   Notice that $d_2 = D_2 / D_1$, exactly as our general formula predicted!
3. **Eigenvalues:** Since $\lambda_1 + \lambda_2 = \operatorname{tr}(S) = a + c$ and $\lambda_1 \lambda_2 = \det(S) = a c - b^2$, having $a > 0$ and $a c - b^2 > 0$ forces $c > \frac{b^2}{a} \ge 0$, so both the sum $\lambda_1 + \lambda_2 > 0$ and the product $\lambda_1 \lambda_2 > 0$ are positive—meaning both eigenvalues $\lambda_1, \lambda_2$ are strictly positive.
4. **Energy (Completing the Square):** Expanding $x^T S x$ for $x = \begin{bmatrix} x_1 & x_2 \end{bmatrix}^T$ and completing the square gives:
   $$
   x^T S x = a x_1^2 + 2 b x_1 x_2 + c x_2^2 = a \left( x_1 + \frac{b}{a} x_2 \right)^2 + \left( c - \frac{b^2}{a} \right) x_2^2
   $$
   Look at the coefficients multiplying the two squared terms: the first coefficient is $d_1 = a$, and the second coefficient is $d_2 = c - \frac{b^2}{a}$! The pivots are literally the weights in the completed square:
   $$
   x^T S x = d_1 \left( x_1 + \ell_{21} x_2 \right)^2 + d_2 x_2^2 \quad \text{where } \ell_{21} = \frac{b}{a}
   $$
   This sum of two squares is strictly positive for all $(x_1, x_2) \ne (0, 0)$ if and only if both pivots are positive ($a > 0$ and $a c - b^2 > 0$).

Having five equivalent tests gives us a huge strategic advantage: whenever we need to prove a theoretical property about positive definite matrices, we can pick whichever test makes the proof easiest! A classic example is proving that the sum of two positive definite matrices is positive definite.

> [!theorem] Sum of Positive Definite Matrices
> If $S_1 \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ and $S_2 \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ are symmetric positive definite ($S_1 \succ 0$ and $S_2 \succ 0$), then their sum $S_1 + S_2$ is also symmetric positive definite ($S_1 + S_2 \succ 0$).

**Proof:**
If we tried to prove $S_1 + S_2 \succ 0$ using eigenvalues (Test 1), determinants (Test 4), or pivots (Test 5), we would immediately run into trouble: when $S_1$ and $S_2$ do not share the same eigenvectors ($S_1 S_2 \ne S_2 S_1$), the eigenvalues, determinants, and pivots of $S_1 + S_2$ are *not* simply the sums of those of $S_1$ and $S_2$.

Instead, let's use the **Energy Test (Test 2)**, which is linear in the matrix $S$! First, $(S_1 + S_2)^T = S_1^T + S_2^T = S_1 + S_2$, so the sum is symmetric. Second, for any non-zero vector $x \in \mathbb{R}^n \setminus \{0\}$:
$$
x^T (S_1 + S_2) x = x^T S_1 x + x^T S_2 x
$$
Because $S_1 \succ 0$ and $S_2 \succ 0$, both individual energies are strictly positive ($x^T S_1 x > 0$ and $x^T S_2 x > 0$). The sum of two positive real numbers is strictly positive:
$$
x^T (S_1 + S_2) x > 0 \quad \text{for all } x \ne 0
$$
Thus, by the Energy Test, $S_1 + S_2 \succ 0$. $\blacksquare$

### Structural Properties of Positive Definite Matrices

When a symmetric matrix $S = S^T$ is positive definite ($S \succ 0$), its entries and its inverse must obey several strict structural constraints:

1. **Positivity of All Diagonal Entries:**
   Every diagonal entry of a positive definite matrix must be strictly positive:
   $$
   s_{ii} > 0 \quad \text{for all } i = 1, 2, \dots, n
   $$
   *Why?* Simply plug the $i$-th standard unit basis vector $x = e_i$ (which has $1$ in position $i$ and $0$ everywhere else) into the Energy Test:
   $$
   e_i^T S e_i = s_{ii} > 0
   $$

2. **Bounds on Off-Diagonal Entries (Maximum Entry Lies on the Diagonal):**
   The largest entry of $S$ in absolute value can *never* hide off the main diagonal—it must sit directly on the main diagonal! Specifically, for every off-diagonal position $i \ne j$:
   $$
   |s_{ij}| < \sqrt{s_{ii} s_{jj}} \le \frac{s_{ii} + s_{jj}}{2} \le \max(s_{ii}, s_{jj}) \quad (i \ne j)
   $$
   *Why?* As we saw earlier, every principal submatrix of a positive definite matrix is positive definite. For any pair of distinct indices $i \ne j$, extract the $2 \times 2$ principal submatrix formed by rows and columns $i$ and $j$:
   $$
   S_{ij} = \begin{bmatrix} s_{ii} & s_{ij} \\ s_{ij} & s_{jj} \end{bmatrix}
   $$
   Because $S_{ij} \succ 0$, its $2 \times 2$ determinant must be strictly positive:
   $$
   \det(S_{ij}) = s_{ii} s_{jj} - s_{ij}^2 > 0 \implies s_{ij}^2 < s_{ii} s_{jj} \implies |s_{ij}| < \sqrt{s_{ii} s_{jj}}
   $$
   Combining this strict inequality with the arithmetic-geometric mean inequality ($\sqrt{s_{ii} s_{jj}} \le \frac{s_{ii} + s_{jj}}{2} \le \max(s_{ii}, s_{jj})$) proves that every off-diagonal entry $|s_{ij}|$ is strictly smaller than the larger of its two corresponding diagonal entries.

3. **Invertibility and Positive Definiteness of the Inverse ($S^{-1} \succ 0$):**
   If $S \succ 0$, then its inverse $S^{-1}$ exists and is also symmetric positive definite:
   - **Existence:** Because all eigenvalues satisfy $\lambda_i(S) > 0$, $\det(S) = \prod_{i=1}^n \lambda_i(S) > 0 \ne 0$, so $S$ is invertible.
   - **Symmetry:** Taking the transpose of the inverse gives $(S^{-1})^T = (S^T)^{-1} = S^{-1}$.
   - **Positive Definiteness:** The eigenvalues of $S^{-1}$ are the reciprocals of the eigenvalues of $S$:
     $$
     \lambda_i(S^{-1}) = \frac{1}{\lambda_i(S)} > 0
     $$
     Since the reciprocal of a positive number is positive, all eigenvalues of $S^{-1}$ are strictly positive! Alternatively, we can see this directly via the Energy Test: for any non-zero vector $y \ne \mathbf{0}$, let $x = S^{-1} y \ne \mathbf{0}$ (so $y = S x$). Then:
     $$
     y^T S^{-1} y = (S x)^T S^{-1} (S x) = x^T S^T S^{-1} S x = x^T S x > 0
     $$

## Cholesky Factorization: $S = R^T R = L_{\text{chol}} L_{\text{chol}}^T$

When we proved the equivalence of the Pivot Test (Test 5) and the $A^T A$ Factorization Test (Test 3), we stumbled upon a remarkably clean triangular factorization. Recall that Gaussian elimination without row exchanges factors any symmetric matrix (with non-zero pivots) into the symmetric form:
$$
S = L D L^T
$$
where $L$ is unit lower triangular and $D = \operatorname{diag}(d_1, \dots, d_n)$ contains the pivots. When $S \succ 0$, all pivots are strictly positive ($d_k > 0$), so we can take the square root of the diagonal pivot matrix $D = D^{1/2} D^{1/2}$ and split it evenly between $L$ and $L^T$:
$$
S = \left(L D^{1/2}\right)\left(D^{1/2} L^T\right)
$$
This symmetrical splitting of $L D L^T$ is called the **Cholesky factorization**—the symmetric positive definite version of $LU$ elimination!

> [!abstract] Definition: Cholesky Factorization
> Every symmetric positive definite matrix $S \succ 0$ admits a **unique** triangular factorization:
> $$
> S = R^T R \quad \left(\text{or equivalently } S = L_{\text{chol}} L_{\text{chol}}^T\right)
> $$
> where $R = D^{1/2} L^T$ is an upper triangular matrix with strictly positive diagonal entries:
> $$
> R_{kk} = \sqrt{d_k} > 0
> $$
> and $L_{\text{chol}} = R^T = L D^{1/2}$ is the corresponding lower triangular Cholesky factor.

### Computational Efficiency and Comparison of Matrix Roots

Notice that both the Cholesky factor $R$ (in $S = R^T R$) and the symmetric matrix square root $S^{1/2}$ (in $S = S^{1/2} S^{1/2}$) act as "square roots" of $S$. How do they compare in practice?

1. **Cholesky Factor $R$ vs. Symmetric Square Root $S^{1/2}$:**
   - **Cholesky Root ($S = R^T R$):** Here $R = D^{1/2} L^T$ is **upper triangular**. Computing $R$ requires zero eigenvalue calculations—it is just standard Gaussian elimination combined with $n$ scalar square roots $\sqrt{d_k}$, and because $S$ is symmetric, we only need to operate on the upper triangle. As a result, Cholesky factorization costs only $\frac{1}{3} n^3$ arithmetic operations—**exact half** the $\frac{2}{3} n^3$ cost of standard $LU$ factorization!
   - **Symmetric Matrix Square Root ($S = S^{1/2} S^{1/2}$):** Constructed from the spectral theorem as:
     $$
     S^{1/2} = Q \Lambda^{1/2} Q^T \quad \text{where } \Lambda^{1/2} = \operatorname{diag}\left(\sqrt{\lambda_1}, \dots, \sqrt{\lambda_n}\right)
     $$
     Because $(S^{1/2})^T = S^{1/2}$ and its eigenvalues are $\sqrt{\lambda_i} > 0$, $S^{1/2}$ is the unique **symmetric positive definite** square root of $S$. However, computing $S^{1/2}$ requires finding all $n$ eigenvalues and orthonormal eigenvectors of $S$, which is vastly more expensive than the $\frac{1}{3} n^3$ Cholesky elimination.

2. **Solving $S x = b$ via Cholesky:**
   In scientific computing, statistics, and optimization, Cholesky factorization is the standard workhorse for solving positive definite linear systems $S x = b$. Once $S = R^T R$ is factored, we solve $R^T R x = b$ in two fast $O(n^2)$ triangular sweeps:
   $$
   R^T y = b \quad (\text{forward substitution}), \qquad R x = y \quad (\text{back substitution})
   $$
   Best of all, because $S \succ 0$, **no row exchanges (pivoting) are ever needed** for numerical stability: the pivots $d_k > 0$ can never vanish, and the diagonal dominance bounds of positive definite matrices prevent dangerous element growth during elimination.

## Positive Semidefinite Matrices ($S \succeq 0$)

What happens on the boundary of positive definiteness, when one or more eigenvalues are allowed to drop all the way to **zero** ($\lambda_i \ge 0$)? Just as positive definite matrices ($S \succ 0$) are the matrix analog of strictly positive numbers ($a > 0$), **positive semidefinite matrices** ($S \succeq 0$) are the matrix analog of non-negative numbers ($a \ge 0$).

> [!abstract] Definition: Positive Semidefinite Matrix
> A real symmetric matrix $S = S^T \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ is **positive semidefinite** (denoted $S \succeq 0$) if its quadratic energy is non-negative for all vectors:
> $$
> x^T S x \ge 0 \quad \text{for all } x \in \mathbb{R}^n
> $$

### Equivalent Characterizations of Positive Semidefiniteness

When we relax strict positivity ($> 0$) to non-negativity ($\ge 0$), how do our five equivalent tests from the Master Theorem adapt? Every strict inequality becomes a weak inequality—with one crucial caveat for determinants and row exchanges!

For any real symmetric matrix $S = S^T \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$, the following conditions are equivalent to $S \succeq 0$:

1. **Eigenvalues ($\lambda_i \ge 0$):** All $n$ eigenvalues of $S$ are non-negative ($\lambda_i \ge 0$, with zero eigenvalues permitted). The proof is identical to the positive definite case: expanding $x = \sum_{i=1}^n c_i q_i$ in the orthonormal eigenbasis gives $x^T S x = \sum_{i=1}^n \lambda_i c_i^2$, which is $\ge 0$ for all $x \in \mathbb{R}^n$ if and only if every $\lambda_i \ge 0$.
2. **Gram Factorization ($S = A^T A$ with Dependent Columns Allowed):** $S$ can be factored as $S = A^T A$ for *any* matrix $A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$—where the columns of $A$ are now allowed to be linearly dependent! Notice how both directions work:
   - If $S = A^T A$ for an arbitrary matrix $A$, then for every $x \in \mathbb{R}^n$:
     $$
     x^T S x = x^T (A^T A) x = (A x)^T (A x) = \|A x\|^2 \ge 0
     $$
     Moreover, since $N(A^T A) = N(A)$, the rank of $S$ equals the rank of $A$: $\operatorname{rank}(S) = \operatorname{rank}(A^T A) = \operatorname{rank}(A)$.
   - Conversely, if $S \succeq 0$, then all eigenvalues satisfy $\lambda_i \ge 0$, so their real square roots $\sqrt{\lambda_i} \ge 0$ still exist! Setting $A = \Lambda^{1/2} Q^T$ (or $A = S^{1/2} = Q \Lambda^{1/2} Q^T$) from the spectral decomposition $S = Q \Lambda Q^T$ immediately gives:
     $$
     A^T A = (Q \Lambda^{1/2})(\Lambda^{1/2} Q^T) = Q \Lambda Q^T = S
     $$
3. **Pivots ($d_k \ge 0$):** Gaussian elimination (potentially requiring symmetric row-and-column permutations $P S P^T$ if a zero appears on the diagonal before elimination finishes) produces only non-negative pivots $d_k \ge 0$, with the number of strictly positive pivots equal to $\operatorname{rank}(S) = r$. Whenever $S = L D L^T$ with $d_k \ge 0$, defining $R = D^{1/2} L^T$ gives $S = R^T R$, linking pivots directly back to $A^T A$.
4. **All Principal Minors Non-Negative:** **Every** principal minor of $S$—not merely the $n$ leading ones!—is non-negative ($\det(S_{\mathcal{I}}) \ge 0$ for all $2^n - 1$ principal submatrices $S_{\mathcal{I}}$). To see why $S \succeq 0$ implies $\det(S_{\mathcal{I}}) \ge 0$, note that restricting $x^T S x \ge 0$ to vectors supported on any index subset $\mathcal{I}$ shows $x_k^T S_{\mathcal{I}} x_k \ge 0$; thus $S_{\mathcal{I}} \succeq 0$, its eigenvalues are all $\ge 0$, and its determinant (the product of its eigenvalues) satisfies $\det(S_{\mathcal{I}}) \ge 0$.

> [!warning] Caution: Leading Principal Minors Are Insufficient for Semidefiniteness!
> Here is one of the most common traps in linear algebra: while checking only the $n$ *leading* upper-left determinants $D_k > 0$ is enough to guarantee positive *definiteness* ($S \succ 0$), checking that the $n$ leading determinants satisfy $D_k \ge 0$ is **not** enough to guarantee positive *semidefiniteness* ($S \succeq 0$)!
> 
> To see why, look at this simple $2 \times 2$ diagonal matrix:
> $$
> S = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & -1 \end{bmatrix}
> $$
> Let's check its leading principal minors:
> - The $1 \times 1$ upper-left determinant is $D_1 = 0 \ge 0$.
> - The $2 \times 2$ determinant is $D_2 = \det(S) = (0)(-1) - 0^2 = 0 \ge 0$.
> 
> Both leading determinants are $\ge 0$, yet the eigenvalues of $S$ are $0$ and $-1$, and testing with $x = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}^T$ gives negative energy $x^T S x = -1 < 0$! What went wrong? The zero in the $(1, 1)$ corner blinded $D_2 = d_1 d_2 = 0 \cdot (-1) = 0$ to the negative entry $-1$ hiding in the $(2, 2)$ corner! Notice that if we check **all** $2^2 - 1 = 3$ principal minors of $S$, the $1 \times 1$ principal minor formed by row 2 and column 2 is $\det([-1]) = -1 < 0$, which immediately catches the negative eigenvalue. For positive semidefiniteness, **all $2^n - 1$ principal minors** must be $\ge 0$.

### Characterization of the Nullspace via Energy

For an arbitrary symmetric matrix $S$ that has both positive and negative eigenvalues (an *indefinite* matrix), the quadratic energy $x^T S x$ can easily equal zero even for a non-zero vector $x$ with $S x \ne 0$ (for example, $S = \operatorname{diag}(1, -1)$ has $x^T S x = x_1^2 - x_2^2 = 0$ along the $45^\circ$ line $x_1 = x_2$, even though $S x \ne 0$).

For a **positive semidefinite** matrix $S \succeq 0$, however, positive and negative eigenvalues cannot cancel each other out because there are no negative eigenvalues! As a result, the only way a vector $x$ can have zero energy ($x^T S x = 0$) is if $x$ sits directly inside the **nullspace** $N(S)$.

> [!theorem] Nullspace and Zero Energy Equivalence
> For any positive semidefinite matrix $S \succeq 0$ and any vector $x \in \mathbb{R}^n$:
> $$
> x^T S x = 0 \iff S x = 0 \iff x \in N(S)
> $$

**Proof:**
- **$(\impliedby)$:** If $x \in N(S)$ so that $S x = 0$, then multiplying on the left by $x^T$ immediately gives $x^T S x = x^T (0) = 0$.
- **$(\implies)$:** Conversely, suppose $x^T S x = 0$. Since $S \succeq 0$, we can factor $S = A^T A$ (for instance, with $A = S^{1/2}$). Substituting $S = A^T A$ into the energy equation turns $x^T S x$ into a squared Euclidean norm:
  $$
  x^T S x = x^T A^T A x = (A x)^T (A x) = \|A x\|^2 = 0
  $$
  The squared length $\|A x\|^2$ of a real vector equals zero if and only if the vector itself is the zero vector:
  $$
  \|A x\|^2 = 0 \implies A x = 0
  $$
  Multiplying both sides on the left by $A^T$ gives $A^T (A x) = 0 \implies S x = 0$, proving that $x \in N(S)$. $\blacksquare$

### Geometry: Bowl vs. Valley

How can we visualize the difference between a positive definite matrix ($S \succ 0$) and a singular positive semidefinite matrix ($S \succeq 0$ with $\det(S) = 0$)? The clearest picture comes from plotting the quadratic energy surface $E(x) = x^T S x$ above the vector space $\mathbb{R}^n$ (and looking at its level sets $x^T S x = 1$):

- **Positive Definite ($S \succ 0$ — Upward Bowl & Closed Ellipsoid):** Because $E(x) = x^T S x > 0$ for every non-zero direction $x \ne 0$, the energy graph curves strictly upward in *every* direction from the origin. Geometrically, $E(x) = x^T S x$ is a strictly convex paraboloid—a **bowl** opening upward—that attains its unique global minimum $E = 0$ at the single isolated point $x = 0$. Slicing this bowl at height $E = 1$ produces the level set $x^T S x = 1$, which is a bounded, closed **ellipsoid** whose principal axes point along the orthonormal eigenvectors $q_i$ with semi-axis lengths $1 / \sqrt{\lambda_i}$.
- **Singular Positive Semidefinite ($S \succeq 0, \det(S) = 0$ — Parabolic Valley & Unbounded Cylinder):** Suppose $S$ has rank $r < n$, so $\lambda_1 \ge \dots \ge \lambda_r > 0$ while the remaining $n - r$ eigenvalues vanish ($\lambda_{r+1} = \dots = \lambda_n = 0$). Along the $r$ positive eigenvector directions, the energy $E(x)$ curves upward just like a bowl; however, along any direction $x \in N(S)$ in the nullspace, the energy stays completely flat at $E(x) = 0$! Geometrically, the energy surface flattens along $N(S)$ into a **parabolic valley (trough)** whose minimum $E = 0$ is attained along the entire nullspace subspace $N(S)$, and the level set $x^T S x = 1$ stretches out indefinitely along $N(S)$ as an unbounded cylinder or pair of parallel bands.

## Matrix Congruence and Sylvester's Law of Inertia

Earlier, when we connected the Pivot Test to the Energy Test via $S = L D L^T$, we noticed something intriguing: even though the diagonal pivot matrix $D$ does *not* have the same eigenvalues as $S$ (because $L^T \ne L^{-1}$, so $S = L D L^T$ is not a similarity transformation), the pivots in $D$ were all positive whenever the eigenvalues of $S$ were all positive.

What kind of transformation is $S \mapsto C^T S C$ when $C$ is an arbitrary invertible matrix, and what spectral property *does* it preserve? This brings us to **congruence transformations** and **Sylvester's Law of Inertia**.

### Congruence Transformations

When we change the basis of a quadratic form $x^T S x$ by substituting $x = C y$ for an invertible matrix $C$, the quadratic form in the new coordinates $y$ becomes:
$$
x^T S x = (C y)^T S (C y) = y^T (C^T S C) y
$$
Notice that the matrix of the quadratic form transforms from $S$ to $C^T S C$ (with $C^T$ on the left, rather than $C^{-1}$ as in a similarity transformation $C^{-1} S C$). Notice also that $(C^T S C)^T = C^T S^T C = C^T S C$, so symmetry is always preserved!

> [!abstract] Definition: Congruence Transformation
> Two real symmetric matrices $S_1$ and $S_2$ are said to be **congruent** if there exists a non-singular (invertible) matrix $C \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ such that:
> $$
> S_2 = C^T S_1 C
> $$

Before we look at matrices with mixed positive and negative eigenvalues, let's check what a transformation of the form $A^T S A$ does to a positive definite or positive semidefinite matrix $S$:

> [!theorem] Preservation of Definiteness under Congruence
> Let $S \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ be a real symmetric matrix.
> 1. If $S \succeq 0$ is positive semidefinite, then for **any** matrix $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times m}$, the symmetric matrix $M = A^T S A$ is positive semidefinite ($M \succeq 0$).
> 2. If $S \succ 0$ is strictly positive definite and $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times m}$ has linearly independent columns ($\operatorname{rank}(A) = m$, so $N(A) = \{0\}$), then $M = A^T S A$ is strictly positive definite ($A^T S A \succ 0$). In particular, any matrix $C^T S C$ congruent to $S \succ 0$ (with $C$ invertible) is strictly positive definite.

**Proof:**
We can prove both statements in two lines using the Energy Test! For any vector $y \in \mathbb{R}^m$, write the quadratic energy of $M = A^T S A$ as:
$$
y^T (A^T S A) y = (A y)^T S (A y) = x^T S x \quad \text{where } x = A y \in \mathbb{R}^n
$$
1. If $S \succeq 0$, then $x^T S x \ge 0$ for every vector $x \in \mathbb{R}^n$, which immediately gives $y^T (A^T S A) y \ge 0$ for all $y \in \mathbb{R}^m$. Thus $A^T S A \succeq 0$.
2. If $S \succ 0$, then $x^T S x > 0$ for every non-zero vector $x \ne 0$. When $A$ has linearly independent columns ($N(A) = \{0\}$), any non-zero vector $y \ne 0$ maps to a non-zero vector $x = A y \ne 0$, which guarantees $y^T (A^T S A) y = x^T S x > 0$. Thus $A^T S A \succ 0$. $\blacksquare$

### Maximal Dimension of Definite Subspaces

What if $S$ is not positive definite across all of $\mathbb{R}^n$, but instead has a mix of positive, negative, and zero eigenvalues? Suppose $S$ has exactly $P$ strictly positive eigenvalues ($\lambda_1 \ge \dots \ge \lambda_P > 0$) and $n - P$ non-positive eigenvalues ($\lambda_{P+1}, \dots, \lambda_n \le 0$).

Clearly, if we restrict our attention to the $P$-dimensional subspace $V_+ = \operatorname{span}\{q_1, \dots, q_P\}$ spanned by the $P$ positive eigenvectors of $S$, the quadratic form $x^T S x = \sum_{i=1}^P \lambda_i c_i^2 > 0$ is strictly positive for every non-zero vector $x \in V_+$. *Could we somehow find an even larger subspace $V_1$—say of dimension $P + 1$—on which $x^T S x > 0$ for all non-zero vectors?* The next theorem proves that the answer is **no**: $P$ is the absolute ceiling on the dimension of any subspace on which $S$ is positive definite!

> [!theorem] Maximal Dimension of Definite Subspaces
> Let $S \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ be a real symmetric matrix with $P$ strictly positive eigenvalues ($\lambda_1 \ge \dots \ge \lambda_P > 0$). If $V_1 \subseteq \mathbb{R}^n$ is any subspace on which $S$ is strictly positive definite:
> $$
> x^T S x > 0 \quad \text{for all } x \in V_1, \; x \ne 0
> $$
> then the dimension of $V_1$ cannot exceed the number of positive eigenvalues of $S$:
> $$
> \dim(V_1) \le P
> $$
> Moreover, this maximal dimension $\dim(V_1) = P$ is attained by the positive eigenspace $V_+ = \operatorname{span}\{q_1, \dots, q_P\}$.

**Proof:**
We can prove this dimension bound cleanly by playing the positive definite subspace $V_1$ against the non-positive eigenspace of $S$ using Grassmann's dimension formula:
1. Let $V_{\le 0}$ be the subspace spanned by all orthonormal eigenvectors of $S$ whose eigenvalues are zero or negative ($\lambda_j \le 0$):
   $$
   V_{\le 0} = \operatorname{span}\{q_j : \lambda_j \le 0\}, \quad \dim(V_{\le 0}) = n - P
   $$
   For any vector $y = \sum_{\lambda_j \le 0} c_j q_j \in V_{\le 0}$, the quadratic form is non-positive:
   $$
   y^T S y = \sum_{\lambda_j \le 0} \lambda_j c_j^2 \le 0
   $$
2. Now let $V_1$ be any subspace on which $S$ is strictly positive definite ($x^T S x > 0$ for all $x \in V_1 \setminus \{0\}$). What can lie in the intersection $V_1 \cap V_{\le 0}$? If there were a non-zero vector $z \in V_1 \cap V_{\le 0}$, then $z \in V_1$ would require $z^T S z > 0$, while $z \in V_{\le 0}$ would require $z^T S z \le 0$—an outright contradiction! Therefore, the two subspaces can intersect only at the origin:
   $$
   V_1 \cap V_{\le 0} = \{0\} \implies \dim(V_1 \cap V_{\le 0}) = 0
   $$
3. By Grassmann's dimension formula for the sum of two subspaces inside $\mathbb{R}^n$:
   $$
   \dim(V_1 + V_{\le 0}) = \dim(V_1) + \dim(V_{\le 0}) - \dim(V_1 \cap V_{\le 0}) \le n
   $$
   Substituting $\dim(V_{\le 0}) = n - P$ and $\dim(V_1 \cap V_{\le 0}) = 0$ immediately gives:
   $$
   \dim(V_1) + (n - P) - 0 \le n \implies \dim(V_1) \le P
   $$
   Finally, the $P$-dimensional positive eigenspace $V_+ = \operatorname{span}\{q_1, \dots, q_P\}$ satisfies $x^T S x = \sum_{i=1}^P \lambda_i c_i^2 > 0$ for all $x \in V_+ \setminus \{0\}$, confirming that the upper bound $P$ is achieved. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Geometric Orientation of Definite Subspaces
> Be careful with one subtle point: while the dimension bound $\dim(V_1) \le P$ is strict, a $P$-dimensional positive definite subspace $V_1$ does **not** have to be the exact eigenspace $V_+$ (nor a subset of $V_+$)!
> 
> Geometrically, you can take the $P$-dimensional subspace $V_+$ and tilt it slightly toward the non-positive eigenspace $V_{\le 0}$; as long as the tilt is small enough that the positive eigenvalue terms strictly outweigh the negative eigenvalue terms for every non-zero vector in $V_1$:
> $$
> \sum_{\lambda_i > 0} \lambda_i c_i^2 > \sum_{\lambda_j < 0} |\lambda_j| c_j^2
> $$
> the tilted $P$-dimensional subspace $V_1$ remains strictly positive definite! Because $V_1 \cap V_{\le 0} = V_1 \cap V_+^\perp = \{0\}$, the orthogonal projection of any such subspace $V_1$ onto $V_+$ has a trivial kernel and is therefore one-to-one, which is the geometric reason why $\dim(V_1)$ can never exceed $\dim(V_+) = P$.

### Sylvester's Law of Inertia

With the Maximal Dimension Theorem in hand, we are ready for one of the most celebrated theorems in matrix theory: **Sylvester's Law of Inertia**. It tells us that even though a congruence transformation $S \mapsto C^T S C$ (with $C$ invertible) stretches and distorts the numerical values of the eigenvalues of $S$, it **cannot change their signs**!

> [!abstract] Definition: Inertia of a Symmetric Matrix
> The **inertia** of a real symmetric matrix $S \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ is the ordered triple of non-negative integers:
> $$
> \operatorname{In}(S) = (P, N, Z)
> $$
> where:
> - $P$ is the number of strictly positive eigenvalues of $S$ ($\lambda_i > 0$),
> - $N$ is the number of strictly negative eigenvalues of $S$ ($\lambda_i < 0$),
> - $Z$ is the number of zero eigenvalues of $S$ ($\lambda_i = 0$),
> 
> counted with multiplicity so that $P + N + Z = n$.

> [!theorem] Sylvester's Law of Inertia
> If $S \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ is a real symmetric matrix and $C \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ is any real non-singular (invertible) matrix, then $S$ and $C^T S C$ have the exact same inertia:
> $$
> \operatorname{In}(C^T S C) = \operatorname{In}(S)
> $$
> That is, congruence transformations preserve the exact counts of positive, negative, and zero eigenvalues.

**Proof:**
Let $M = C^T S C$. Suppose $S$ has $P$ positive eigenvalues and $M$ has $P'$ positive eigenvalues. 

We first start with the subspace $V_1$ spanned by the positive eigenvectors of $M$ (the eigenvectors corresponding to the $P'$ positive eigenvalues of $M$), which has dimension $\dim(V_1) = P'$. Since $V_1$ is spanned by the positive eigenvectors of $M$, for any non-zero vector $y \in V_1$, we have $y^T M y > 0$. Writing $M = C^T S C$, this gives:
$$
y^T C^T S C y > 0 \implies (C y)^T S (C y) > 0
$$
Notice that the vector $C y$ belongs to the transformed subspace $C V_1 = \{C y : y \in V_1\}$. Because $C$ is invertible, multiplying by $C$ preserves linear independence, so $C V_1$ has the exact same dimension as $V_1$, which is $P'$. Furthermore, since $(C y)^T S (C y) > 0$ for every non-zero vector in $C V_1$, the subspace $C V_1$ is a positive definite subspace of $S$. 

By the Maximal Dimension Theorem, the dimension of any positive definite subspace of $S$ cannot exceed $P$, so we immediately have:
$$
P' \le P
$$
Now we can repeat the exact same argument the other way around by writing $S = D^T M D$, where $D = C^{-1}$. Starting with the $P$-dimensional subspace spanned by the positive eigenvectors of $S$ and mapping it through $D$ gives a $P$-dimensional positive definite subspace of $M$, which implies:
$$
P \le P'
$$
Combining both inequalities gives $P' = P$, so $M$ and $S$ have the exact same number of positive eigenvalues.

Applying the same reasoning to $-S$ (where negative eigenvalues become positive) shows that the number of negative eigenvalues is also preserved ($N' = N$). Finally, since the total number of eigenvalues is $n = P + N + Z$, the number of zero eigenvalues must match as well:
$$
Z' = n - P' - N' = n - P - N = Z
$$
This establishes $\operatorname{In}(C^T S C) = \operatorname{In}(S)$. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Invariance of Signs: Eigenvalues vs. Pivots
> Because the symmetric elimination factorization $S = L D L^T$ is a congruence transformation ($C = L^T$ is invertible, with $L^{-1}$ existing):
> $$
> D = L^{-1} S (L^{-1})^T
> $$
> the diagonal pivot matrix $D$ has the exact same inertia as $S$:
> - Number of positive pivots in $D$ = Number of positive eigenvalues of $S$ ($P$).
> - Number of negative pivots in $D$ = Number of negative eigenvalues of $S$ ($N$).
> - Number of zero pivots in $D$ = Number of zero eigenvalues of $S$ ($Z$).
> 
> While elimination does not preserve the individual eigenvalues ($\lambda_i(D) \ne \lambda_i(S)$), it strictly preserves their signs. This property provides an efficient $O(n^3)$ algorithm for counting the number of eigenvalues in any interval $[a, b]$ by counting the negative pivots of shifted matrices $S - a I$ and $S - b I$.

### The Rayleigh Quotient

When we study the quadratic energy $x^T S x$ of a symmetric matrix $S$, there is an obvious catch: if we simply scale the vector $x$ to be ten times longer, the energy $x^T S x$ becomes a hundred times larger, even though the direction of $x$ hasn't changed at all. What if we want to measure how much $S$ stretches or compresses space purely as a function of *direction*, independent of vector length? To do that, we normalize the quadratic energy by dividing by the squared length $x^T x = \|x\|^2$. This normalized ratio is called the **Rayleigh quotient**.

> [!abstract] Definition: Rayleigh Quotient
> For a real symmetric matrix $S = S^T \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ and any non-zero vector $x \in \mathbb{R}^n \setminus \{0\}$, the **Rayleigh quotient** is the scalar ratio:
> $$
> R(x) = \frac{x^T S x}{x^T x}
> $$

#### Scale Invariance (Degree-Zero Homogeneity)

Let's check what happens if we scale $x$ by any non-zero scalar $c \ne 0$:
$$
R(c x) = \frac{(c x)^T S (c x)}{(c x)^T (c x)} = \frac{c^2 (x^T S x)}{c^2 (x^T x)} = R(x)
$$
Notice that the factor $c^2$ cancels out completely! Because $R(x)$ depends *only* on the direction of $x$ and is completely invariant under scaling, we never actually need to search over all of $\mathbb{R}^n \setminus \{0\}$ when maximizing or minimizing $R(x)$. Every direction is already represented by a unit vector $u$ with $\|u\| = 1$, where the denominator $u^T u = 1$ disappears. In other words, unconstrained optimization of $R(x)$ over all non-zero vectors is equivalent to constrained optimization of the quadratic form $u^T S u$ on the unit sphere:
$$
\max_{x \ne 0} R(x) = \max_{\|u\| = 1} u^T S u, \qquad \min_{x \ne 0} R(x) = \min_{\|u\| = 1} u^T S u
$$

#### Spectral Bounds and Convex Combination

Now let's ask a natural question: *As $x$ points in different directions across $\mathbb{R}^n$, how large or small can the Rayleigh quotient $R(x)$ get?* 

Because $S$ is symmetric, the Spectral Theorem gives us an orthonormal eigenbasis $\{q_1, q_2, \dots, q_n\}$ with $S q_i = \lambda_i q_i$, where we can order the real eigenvalues from largest to smallest: $\lambda_1 \ge \lambda_2 \ge \dots \ge \lambda_n$. If we expand any non-zero vector $x$ in this orthonormal eigenbasis, its squared length is simply the sum of the squared coordinates:
$$
x = \sum_{i=1}^n c_i q_i \implies \|x\|^2 = x^T x = \sum_{i=1}^n c_i^2
$$
At the same time, because the eigenvectors are orthonormal ($q_i^T q_j = \delta_{ij}$), all cross-terms vanish when we compute the quadratic energy:
$$
x^T S x = \left(\sum_{i=1}^n c_i q_i\right)^T \left(\sum_{j=1}^n c_j \lambda_j q_j\right) = \sum_{i=1}^n \lambda_i c_i^2
$$
Substituting both of these expressions into the Rayleigh quotient reveals something very clean:
$$
R(x) = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^n \lambda_i c_i^2}{\sum_{i=1}^n c_i^2} = \sum_{i=1}^n w_i \lambda_i, \quad \text{where } w_i = \frac{c_i^2}{\sum_{j=1}^n c_j^2} \ge 0, \; \sum_{i=1}^n w_i = 1
$$
Let's pause and appreciate what this equation is telling us: for *any* vector $x$, the Rayleigh quotient $R(x)$ is just a **weighted average** (a convex combination) of the eigenvalues $\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_n$! And since a weighted average of numbers can never be larger than the largest number or smaller than the smallest number, the extreme eigenvalues immediately trap $R(x)$ from above and below.

> [!theorem] Spectral Bounds on the Rayleigh Quotient
> For any real symmetric matrix $S$ with ordered eigenvalues $\lambda_1 \ge \lambda_2 \ge \dots \ge \lambda_n$, the Rayleigh quotient of any non-zero vector $x \ne 0$ is bounded between the extreme eigenvalues:
> $$
> \lambda_n \le R(x) \le \lambda_1
> $$
> Moreover, these bounds are sharp:
> $$
> \max_{x \ne 0} R(x) = \lambda_1 \quad (\text{attained at } x = q_1), \qquad \min_{x \ne 0} R(x) = \lambda_n \quad (\text{attained at } x = q_n)
> $$

**Proof:**
Since each eigenvalue satisfies $\lambda_n \le \lambda_i \le \lambda_1$, multiplying through by the non-negative weights $w_i \ge 0$ and summing from $i = 1$ to $n$ gives:
$$
\lambda_n = \sum_{i=1}^n w_i \lambda_n \le \sum_{i=1}^n w_i \lambda_i = R(x) \le \sum_{i=1}^n w_i \lambda_1 = \lambda_1
$$
When do we hit the upper bound $R(x) = \lambda_1$? To make a weighted average equal to the maximum value $\lambda_1$, we must put zero weight ($c_i = 0$) on every strictly smaller eigenvalue $\lambda_i < \lambda_1$. That happens if and only if $x$ lies entirely in the eigenspace $E_{\lambda_1}$ corresponding to $\lambda_1$ (for instance, choosing $x = q_1$). By the exact same logic, $R(x) = \lambda_n$ if and only if $x \in E_{\lambda_n}$ (attained at $x = q_n$). $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Stationary Points and Eigenvectors
> What happens if we look at $R(x)$ through the lens of multivariable calculus and search for its critical (stationary) points? Differentiating $R(x)$ with respect to the vector $x$ using the quotient rule gives:
> $$
> \nabla R(x) = \frac{2 S x (x^T x) - 2 x (x^T S x)}{(x^T x)^2} = \frac{2}{x^T x} \left( S x - R(x) x \right)
> $$
> Setting the gradient equal to zero yields the stationarity condition:
> $$
> \nabla R(x) = 0 \iff S x = R(x) x
> $$
> Look at that right-hand equation: $S x = R(x) x$ is literally the eigenvalue equation $S x = \lambda x$! In other words, the stationary points of the Rayleigh quotient are precisely the **eigenvectors** of $S$, and the values of $R(x)$ at those stationary points are the **eigenvalues** $\lambda_i$. Geometrically, $\lambda_1$ is the global maximum, $\lambda_n$ is the global minimum, and all intermediate eigenvalues $\lambda_2, \dots, \lambda_{n-1}$ correspond to saddle points on the unit sphere.

### The Courant-Fischer Minimax Theorem

We now know how to find the largest eigenvalue $\lambda_1$ (maximize $R(x)$) and the smallest eigenvalue $\lambda_n$ (minimize $R(x)$). *But how can we characterize an intermediate eigenvalue $\lambda_k$ purely through optimization, without having to compute the first $k - 1$ eigenvectors beforehand?*

The **Courant-Fischer Minimax Theorem** answers this beautifully by turning the search for $\lambda_k$ into a two-step minimax game over subspaces. Imagine you get to choose a $k$-dimensional subspace $V \subseteq \mathbb{R}^n$, and once you pick $V$, an adversary picks a non-zero vector $x \in V$ to make $R(x)$ as *small* as possible. Your goal is to pick the $k$-dimensional subspace $V$ that makes this worst-case minimum as *large* as possible. Courant and Fischer showed that the exact value of this game is $\lambda_k$.

> [!theorem] Courant-Fischer Minimax Theorem
> Let $S \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ be a real symmetric matrix with eigenvalues arranged in non-increasing order:
> $$
> \lambda_1 \ge \lambda_2 \ge \dots \ge \lambda_n
> $$
> Then for every $k \in \{1, 2, \dots, n\}$, the $k$-th eigenvalue $\lambda_k$ satisfies the variational minimax characterization:
> $$
> \lambda_k = \max_{\dim(V) = k} \; \min_{x \in V, \, x \ne 0} \frac{x^T S x}{x^T x} = \min_{\dim(W) = n - k + 1} \; \max_{x \in W, \, x \ne 0} \frac{x^T S x}{x^T x}
> $$
> where $V$ and $W$ denote subspaces of $\mathbb{R}^n$.

**Proof:**
Let $\{q_1, q_2, \dots, q_n\}$ be an orthonormal eigenbasis of $\mathbb{R}^n$ corresponding to the ordered eigenvalues $\lambda_1 \ge \dots \ge \lambda_n$, so that $S q_i = \lambda_i q_i$ and $q_i^T q_j = \delta_{ij}$. To prove that the maximum over all $k$-dimensional subspaces $V$ of $\min_{x \in V, x \ne 0} R(x)$ equals $\lambda_k$, we will bound this quantity from below and from above.

1. **Lower Bound (Finding a subspace that achieves at least $\lambda_k$):**
   To show that the maximum over all $k$-dimensional subspaces is at least $\lambda_k$, we just need to exhibit one specific $k$-dimensional subspace where $R(x) \ge \lambda_k$ for every vector. Let's pick the subspace spanned by the first $k$ eigenvectors:
   $$
   V_k = \operatorname{span}\{q_1, q_2, \dots, q_k\}, \quad \dim(V_k) = k
   $$
   Any non-zero vector $x \in V_k$ is a combination $x = \sum_{i=1}^k c_i q_i$ with $\|x\|^2 = \sum_{i=1}^k c_i^2 > 0$. Since every eigenvalue in this subspace satisfies $\lambda_i \ge \lambda_k$ (for $i = 1, \dots, k$), its quadratic energy is bounded below by:
   $$
   x^T S x = \sum_{i=1}^k \lambda_i c_i^2 \ge \lambda_k \sum_{i=1}^k c_i^2 = \lambda_k \|x\|^2
   $$
   Dividing by $\|x\|^2$ tells us that $R(x) \ge \lambda_k$ for all non-zero $x \in V_k$. Furthermore, if we plug in $x = q_k \in V_k$, we get $R(q_k) = \lambda_k$ exactly. Therefore, the minimum of $R(x)$ on this specific subspace $V_k$ is $\lambda_k$:
   $$
   \min_{x \in V_k, \, x \ne 0} R(x) = \lambda_k
   $$
   Since the maximum over *all* $k$-dimensional subspaces $V$ must be at least as large as the value achieved by $V_k$, we obtain the lower bound:
   $$
   \max_{\dim(V) = k} \; \min_{x \in V, \, x \ne 0} R(x) \ge \min_{x \in V_k, \, x \ne 0} R(x) = \lambda_k
   $$

2. **Upper Bound (Why no $k$-dimensional subspace can do better than $\lambda_k$):**
   Now let $V$ be *any* $k$-dimensional subspace of $\mathbb{R}^n$. We want to show that no matter how cleverly $V$ is tilted, it must contain at least one direction $z$ where $R(z) \le \lambda_k$. To trap such a vector, consider the $(n - k + 1)$-dimensional subspace spanned by the remaining eigenvectors from $q_k$ down to $q_n$:
   $$
   W_{n - k + 1} = \operatorname{span}\{q_k, q_{k+1}, \dots, q_n\}, \quad \dim(W_{n - k + 1}) = n - k + 1
   $$
   Look at the dimensions of $V$ and $W_{n - k + 1}$: their dimensions add up to $k + (n - k + 1) = n + 1$, which is strictly greater than the dimension $n$ of the entire space $\mathbb{R}^n$! By Grassmann's dimension formula:
   $$
   \dim(V \cap W_{n - k + 1}) = \dim(V) + \dim(W_{n - k + 1}) - \dim(V + W_{n - k + 1}) \ge k + (n - k + 1) - n = 1
   $$
   Because the intersection has dimension at least $1$, the two subspaces cannot just meet at the origin—there must be at least one non-zero vector $z \ne 0$ that lies in $V \cap W_{n - k + 1}$.
   
   Since $z \in W_{n - k + 1}$, we can expand it using only the eigenvectors from $q_k$ to $q_n$: $z = \sum_{j=k}^n d_j q_j$. Every eigenvalue in this list satisfies $\lambda_j \le \lambda_k$, which forces:
   $$
   z^T S z = \sum_{j=k}^n \lambda_j d_j^2 \le \lambda_k \sum_{j=k}^n d_j^2 = \lambda_k \|z\|^2 \implies R(z) \le \lambda_k
   $$
   At the same time, $z$ also belongs to $V$! So the minimum of $R(x)$ over all non-zero vectors in $V$ cannot possibly be larger than $R(z)$:
   $$
   \min_{x \in V, \, x \ne 0} R(x) \le R(z) \le \lambda_k
   $$
   Since this holds for every $k$-dimensional subspace $V$, taking the maximum over all such subspaces gives the upper bound:
   $$
   \max_{\dim(V) = k} \; \min_{x \in V, \, x \ne 0} R(x) \le \lambda_k
   $$

3. **Conclusion:**
   Combining the lower bound and the upper bound forces equality:
   $$
   \max_{\dim(V) = k} \; \min_{x \in V, \, x \ne 0} R(x) = \lambda_k
   $$
   Finally, applying the exact same result to $-S$ (which flips the order of the eigenvalues and turns minimums into maximums) gives the dual min-max characterization $\min_{\dim(W) = n - k + 1} \max_{x \in W, x \ne 0} R(x) = \lambda_k$. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Alternative Proof: Maximal Dimension of Definite Subspaces via Minimax
> Notice how quickly the Courant-Fischer Minimax Theorem gives a second, independent proof of the **Maximal Dimension Theorem** from earlier!
> 
> Suppose $V_1$ is a $k$-dimensional subspace on which $S$ is strictly positive definite ($x^T S x > 0$ for all $x \in V_1 \setminus \{0\}$). Because the unit sphere in $V_1$ is closed and bounded (compact), the continuous Rayleigh quotient attains a strictly positive minimum value $\epsilon > 0$ on $V_1$:
> $$
> \min_{x \in V_1, \, x \ne 0} R(x) = \epsilon > 0
> $$
> Plugging $V_1$ into the Courant-Fischer characterization of the $k$-th eigenvalue immediately tells us:
> $$
> \lambda_k = \max_{\dim(V) = k} \; \min_{x \in V, \, x \ne 0} R(x) \ge \min_{x \in V_1, \, x \ne 0} R(x) = \epsilon > 0
> $$
> Since our eigenvalues are ordered $\lambda_1 \ge \lambda_2 \ge \dots \ge \lambda_k > 0$, having $\lambda_k > 0$ means at least the first $k$ eigenvalues of $S$ are strictly positive! Therefore, the total number of positive eigenvalues $P$ must satisfy $P \ge k = \dim(V_1)$, or $\dim(V_1) \le P$.

## The Geometry of Quadratic Forms: The Ellipsoid

### Canonical Form via Orthogonal Diagonalization

Earlier we saw that for a symmetric positive definite matrix $S = S^T \succ 0$, the graph of the energy function $E(x) = x^T S x$ is a bowl opening upward. What does a horizontal cross-section of this bowl look like? If we slice the bowl at height $1$, the resulting level surface
$$
x^T S x = 1
$$
defines a **hyper-ellipsoid** centered at the origin in $\mathbb{R}^n$.

When $S$ has off-diagonal entries, the expanded equation $x^T S x = 1$ contains mixed cross-terms like $2 s_{12} x_1 x_2$, which means the ellipsoid is tilted relative to the standard coordinate axes. How do we untilt it? We use the Spectral Theorem, $S = Q \Lambda Q^T$! Substituting this into the level set equation gives:
$$
x^T S x = x^T (Q \Lambda Q^T) x = (Q^T x)^T \Lambda (Q^T x) = 1
$$
This motivates defining a new coordinate vector $X$ by rotating our coordinate axes to align with the orthonormal eigenvectors of $S$:
$$
X = Q^T x = \begin{bmatrix} X_1 \\ X_2 \\ \vdots \\ X_n \end{bmatrix} \iff x = Q X = \sum_{i=1}^n X_i q_i
$$
In these new **principal coordinates** $X_1, \dots, X_n$, every single cross-term vanishes, leaving the clean canonical equation of an axis-aligned ellipsoid:
$$
X^T \Lambda X = \lambda_1 X_1^2 + \lambda_2 X_2^2 + \cdots + \lambda_n X_n^2 = 1
$$

> [!theorem] The Principal Axis Theorem
> For the ellipsoid $x^T S x = 1$ defined by a symmetric positive definite matrix $S \succ 0$:
> 1. **Axis Directions:** The principal axes of the ellipsoid point along the orthonormal eigenvectors $q_1, q_2, \dots, q_n$ of $S$.
> 2. **Axis Half-Lengths (Semi-Axes):** Moving purely along the $i$-th eigenvector $q_i$ (setting $X_j = 0$ for all $j \ne i$) gives $\lambda_i X_i^2 = 1 \implies X_i = \pm \frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_i}}$. Thus, the semi-axis half-length along $q_i$ is:
>    $$
>    a_i = \frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_i}}
>    $$
> 3. **Curvature Inversion:**
>    - The **largest eigenvalue** $\lambda_{\max}$ corresponds to the **shortest semi-axis** of length $1/\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}}$ (the direction of steepest upward curvature).
>    - The **smallest eigenvalue** $\lambda_{\min}$ corresponds to the **longest semi-axis** of length $1/\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}}$ (the direction of slowest ascent / flattest profile).

Why does a *larger* eigenvalue give a *shorter* axis? Think about the energy bowl $x^T S x$: along an eigenvector with a large eigenvalue $\lambda_{\max}$, the energy climbs very steeply, so you don't have to walk very far from the origin before your energy reaches $1$. Along an eigenvector with a small eigenvalue $\lambda_{\min}$, the bowl is much flatter, so you have to travel much farther out—distance $1/\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}}$—before reaching height $1$.

![[06. Eigenvalues and Eigenvectors-1792298570405.webp]]
![[06. Eigenvalues and Eigenvectors-1792474259550.webp]]

For example, the figure above illustrates the tilted 3D ellipsoid $x^T S x = 1$ for the positive definite matrix:
$$
S = \begin{bmatrix}
3 & 1 & 0 \\
1 & 2 & 1 \\
0 & 1 & 2
\end{bmatrix}
$$
Its three orthogonal axes point along the eigenvectors $q_1, q_2, q_3$ of $S$, with half-lengths $1/\sqrt{\lambda_1}$, $1/\sqrt{\lambda_2}$, and $1/\sqrt{\lambda_3}$.

## Calculus, Multivariable Optimization, and Machine Learning

Why do positive definite matrices play such a central role in multivariable calculus, optimization, and machine learning? Because whenever we zoom in on a smooth function near a minimum or maximum, its local behavior is governed by a symmetric quadratic form!

### Multivariable Second Derivative Test

In single-variable calculus, when we find a critical point $f'(x^*) = 0$, we check the sign of the second derivative $f''(x^*)$ to see if the curve bends upward ($f''(x^*) > 0$, a local minimum) or downward ($f''(x^*) < 0$, a local maximum). How does this generalize to a function $f: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}$ of $n$ variables?
